### Source file 29: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/02_Master_Prompts/Manus_AI_ReadMeHub_Full_Elementor_Build_Master_Prompt.md`

# Manus AI Master Build Prompt — ReadMeHub WordPress + Elementor (Free-First)

## Mission

Rebuild the uploaded ReadMeHub website design into a complete, working, editable, transfer-ready WordPress website. Use both supplied files as required project inputs:

1. **Visual reference ZIP:** `website-1e7d1110-c3bf-4e84-99e1-05be4a65fe5c.zip` — contains `index.html`, a static HTML/Tailwind design reference. It is **not** an Elementor kit, WordPress theme, or importable WordPress website. Inspect the actual markup, CSS/Tailwind configuration, images, icons, responsive behavior, header, footer, and every visible section. Rebuild its visual design in WordPress rather than merely importing or embedding the HTML.
2. **Functional/content specification:** `ReadMeHub — Elementor Free-First Website Specification.md` — use this as the required architecture, content-model, reusable-component, responsive, accessibility, trust, QA, and handoff specification. Read the complete file, not just the copy-ready prompt near the end.

The final website must retain the ZIP's visual identity and reproduce its header and footer as closely as the target stack permits, while following the specification's implementation and editorial principles. It must include complete, coherent content and functioning WordPress admin editing—not just a mockup, screenshot, static HTML page, or design proposal.

## 1. Source-of-truth and conflict rules

Use this authority order:

1. **ZIP HTML is the visual source of truth** for its design language, logo/wordmark treatment, header/footer appearance, visual hierarchy, layout patterns, card styles, colors, typography, icons, borders, shadows, radii, spacing, and desktop/mobile composition.
2. **The Markdown specification is the source of truth** for information architecture principles, component ownership, WordPress/Elementor constraints, content modeling, accessibility, responsive testing, evidence rules, reusable patterns, and quality gates.
3. **This prompt's explicit requirements** extend the site with complete blog, category/archive, comparison, deal/coupon, single deal/coupon, and custom admin-field functionality.
4. If a requirement conflicts, do not silently discard it. Record the conflict, use the above hierarchy, and explain the implementation decision in the handoff.

Do not redesign the site into an unrelated visual style. Do not substitute a generic theme demo. Do not make up a new design system when the ZIP already defines one. Preserve the visual identity but fix genuine accessibility, responsiveness, semantic, performance, and content-trust problems without gratuitously changing the appearance.

### Extract and preserve the ZIP design tokens

At minimum, inspect and document the values actually used in the HTML. Initial values visible in its Tailwind configuration include:

- Brand navy: `#0F2A4A`
- Dark navy: `#0A1C33`
- Navy/light blue: `#1E3A8A`
- Brand orange: `#FF7A00`
- Orange hover: `#E66E00`
- Light orange: `#FFF4EB`
- Pastel blue: `#F0F5FA`
- Pastel mint: `#EFFBFA`
- Pastel purple: `#F5F3FF`
- Gray background: `#F8FAFC`
- Slate text: `#334155`
- Muted text: `#64748B`
- Body font: `Inter`
- Display/headings font: `Plus Jakarta Sans`
- Soft shadow: `0 4px 20px -2px rgba(15, 42, 74, 0.06)`
- Card shadow: `0 10px 30px -4px rgba(15, 42, 74, 0.08)`
- Hover shadow: `0 20px 35px -5px rgba(15, 42, 74, 0.12)`
- Orange glow: `0 8px 24px -4px rgba(255, 122, 0, 0.35)`

These are starting values observed in the HTML; inspect the entire file and use the exact source values wherever defined. Do not assume this short list captures every token. Create a token inventory for color, type scale, line-height, spacing, max-widths, border, radius, shadow, icon treatment, focus state, breakpoints, transitions, and light/dark theme. Keep tokens centralized in Elementor Site Settings/global styles and documented, scoped CSS variables where needed. Do not scatter inconsistent one-off values through pages.

**Visual parity requirements:**
- Recreate the ZIP header, navigation, wordmark/subtitle, desktop dropdown/mega-menu patterns if present, search, theme control, CTA, mobile menu, and their actual states. The header should look and behave as close to the ZIP as practical.
- Recreate the ZIP footer's layout, brand summary, link columns, social links/icons, and spacing. Keep the same visual structure, but only publish links and claims that are valid and approved.
- Recreate all relevant page sections from the source reference using editable native widgets/containers. Do not use a screenshot as the page, a single giant HTML widget, or a single flattened image.
- Match desktop, tablet, mobile, hover, focus, open/closed, and light/dark states where they exist in the reference.
- If a precise visual detail cannot be reproduced with the verified free stack, document the exact limitation and use the smallest maintainable workaround.

## 2. Required expert roles

Operate as a coordinated senior implementation team. Apply each role's review independently and resolve disagreements before release.

1. **Lead WordPress Solutions Architect** — WordPress structure, theme/plugin boundaries, portability, update safety, deployment strategy.
2. **Senior Elementor Free Architect** — native widget/container composition, editable layouts, Site Settings, global styles, reusable section strategy, verified compatibility.
3. **Xpro Addons Compatibility Specialist** — inspect the installed/available Xpro version and license; use only widgets/features actually available in the free tier. Never assume a Pro-only widget is included.
4. **Reference Design Reverse-Engineering Specialist** — inspect the ZIP HTML and reconstruct design tokens, component anatomy, page layout, responsive rules, and interactions.
5. **UI/UX and Information Architecture Lead** — blog publication experience, navigation, category/archive discovery, comparison and coupon/deal user journeys.
6. **WordPress Theme Developer** — only if needed, build the smallest maintainable theme/child-theme integration to achieve the reference header/footer and template requirements.
7. **WordPress Plugin and Content-Model Architect** — CPTs, taxonomies, ACF field groups, validation, admin usability, export/migration behavior.
8. **ACF Free Custom-Field Specialist** — design practical, clearly labeled editor fields without assuming paid Repeater/Flexible Content features.
9. **Editorial SEO and Structured Data Specialist** — canonical URLs, archive strategy, metadata, internal linking, schema only where accurate and eligible.
10. **Editorial Content Strategist and Fact-Checker** — produce complete useful starter content, verify external facts and offers, maintain source records, avoid fabricated claims.
11. **Affiliate/Coupon Operations Specialist** — offer lifecycle, coupon validity, terms, destination, affiliate disclosure, expiration and verification states.
12. **Responsive Frontend QA Engineer** — test real desktop/tablet/mobile widths, intermediate breakpoints, reflow, overflow, interactions.
13. **Accessibility Reviewer** — semantic HTML, keyboard access, focus, contrast, form labels/errors, reduced motion, non-color status cues.
14. **Performance and Security Engineer** — lean asset loading, image optimization, safe output escaping/sanitization, permissions, update-safe code.
15. **Migration/Release Engineer** — package files, dependencies, installation/import instructions, rollback and clean-site validation.
16. **Red-Team / Acceptance-Test Reviewer** — independently challenge assumptions, find broken states, test admin editing and transfer readiness.

Do not just list these roles in the report: actually use them as sequential review passes, record findings, fix critical issues, and show unresolved items.

## 3. Inspect the actual target before choosing an implementation

First inventory:
- WordPress version and PHP version, if a target site exists.
- Elementor version and editor generation (Free; V3/V4/hybrid as applicable).
- Hello Elementor/theme version, active theme, child theme, active plugins, and Xpro Addons exact version/license.
- ACF version/license and existing field groups.
- Existing Elementor Site Settings, global colors/fonts, templates, custom CSS, menus, permalinks, media, and content.
- Available hosting/server restrictions, writable directories, upload limits, and packaging/import constraints.
- Whether the deliverable is being built in a live target, staging site, or isolated workspace.

Never claim a version, plugin capability, import, or test is confirmed until inspected. If there is no WordPress target access, create the complete installable package and a precise verification checklist; clearly label runtime tests as **NOT RUN** instead of pretending they passed.

### Theme decision — native-first, not dogmatic

Preferred order:
1. Elementor Free native containers/widgets and WordPress content.
2. Verified free Xpro widgets only where they add a necessary capability.
3. Hello Elementor-compatible, update-safe child-theme or minimal template integration where necessary.
4. A minimal standalone custom theme **only if** the verified stack cannot reproduce the reference header/footer/templates reliably and a child-theme/template approach is unsuitable.
5. A small site-specific functionality plugin for custom post types/taxonomies and durable site functionality, where appropriate.

Do not create a new theme merely for the sake of it. Do not replace Hello Elementor without explaining why. If custom theme work is necessary, keep it lean, documented, accessible, Elementor-compatible, and free of content/data lock-in. Keep CPTs/taxonomies in a functionality plugin rather than tying them to a visual theme. Do not duplicate the same behavior in both theme and plugin.

## 4. Required pages and templates

Build and populate all these core page families, not just the homepage:

1. **Homepage** — reproduce the reference's section hierarchy and visual design; adapt content to a credible publication and working routes.
2. **Blog index** — polished post listing with pagination or a verified load-more alternative, featured/most useful content, search/filter only if it works, and a meaningful empty state.
3. **Single blog post** — title, dek/excerpt, author, published/updated dates, category, featured image, table of contents if useful, readable article body, related posts, sources/references where claims need them, disclosure where relevant, newsletter CTA, and correction/report route.
4. **Category archive** — category title/description, real post cards, pagination, internal links, correct archive title/meta, and no empty or fabricated cards.
5. **Comparison index** — comparison cards with clear decision intent, summary, compared options, and link to each single comparison.
6. **Single comparison page** — concise verdict only when evidence supports it, best-for/use cases, criteria, side-by-side option cards or a responsive comparison layout, trade-offs, pricing only when sourced and dated, limitations, methodology, last-verified status, sources, alternatives, related articles, and visible affiliate disclosure if applicable. Never require horizontal scrolling on mobile.
7. **Deals/Coupons index** — filter/group by actual taxonomy or status only if implemented and useful; display verified offer details, validity, terms summary, destination, disclosure, and expiration/expired state. No fake countdown timers, false urgency, or invented discounts.
8. **Single deal/coupon page** — offer title, merchant/product, code only if real and confirmed, offer terms, who is eligible, exclusions, valid-from/expiry if known, last-checked date and source, clear destination link, disclosure, related guides/comparisons, and a clear expired/unverified state.
9. **Search results** — meaningful query, result count if accurate, no-results guidance, accessible form, and real WordPress search behavior.
10. **About** — factual purpose and editorial approach; no invented founder bio, credentials, company history, or testing claims.
11. **Methodology / Editorial Standards** — explain what the site actually does and what it does not claim to do.
12. **Affiliate Disclosure** — plain-language, visible and accurate.
13. **Contact / Report an Error** — working route/form or a clear contact method, with validation and error/success states if a form exists.
14. **Privacy Policy and Terms** — create editable draft pages for owner/legal review. Do not claim legal approval or jurisdictional compliance without review.
15. **404 page** — branded, helpful navigation and search.

Create only additional use-case/product hubs when content inventory justifies them. Do not leave public navigation linking to empty pages.

## 5. WordPress content model and admin custom fields

The WordPress dashboard must allow a non-developer editor to add, edit, update, expire, and verify content without touching code. Create clearly labeled menu items and field groups.

### A. Blog posts

Use the native WordPress `Posts` type unless a documented reason requires otherwise. Add/confirm:
- Standard title, slug, editor content, excerpt, featured image, author, publish date, categories, tags.
- Optional structured ACF fields only where genuinely useful: short dek/subtitle, editorial content type, source/methodology note, last reviewed date, reviewer/editor, evidence status, affiliate relationship flag, and related comparison/deal references if the field type is verified.
- Preserve the normal WordPress editor for the main article body; do not move the entire article into dozens of custom fields.
- Provide helpful field instructions, sensible optional/required rules, and neutral fallbacks.

### B. Comparisons — custom post type

Register a CPT such as `Comparisons` with:
- Public, searchable, archive-enabled configuration; clean rewrite slug such as `/compare/` or `/comparisons/` selected consistently and documented.
- Native title/editor/excerpt/featured image, author, dates, revision support, REST/editor compatibility as appropriate.
- Taxonomies only when useful, e.g. comparison category and topic.
- ACF fields, with actual field types/keys documented, such as:
  - Option A name and canonical URL
  - Option B name and canonical URL
  - One-line decision summary
  - Best for / not ideal for
  - Comparison status: Draft, Needs verification, Verified, Update due, Archived
  - Evidence/methodology note
  - Last verified date and source URL
  - Pricing status and pricing-source URL/date (never assume a price)
  - Affiliate CTA destination per option only when approved and verified
  - Disclosure requirement/relationship status
  - Related posts and related deals where compatible field types exist
- Use normal editor content for detailed reasoning, limitations, criteria, and prose. ACF Free lacks some repeater-style field types; do not assume Repeater/Flexible Content exists. If repeatable criteria are needed, choose a maintainable editor-content structure or a clearly bounded number of simple fields and explain the trade-off.
- Comparison archive cards must derive from actual published CPT records, not hard-coded fake cards.

### C. Deals/Coupons — custom post type

Register a CPT such as `Deals & Coupons` with:
- Public archive, clean rewrite slug such as `/deals/` or `/coupons/`, searchable where appropriate, native editor/excerpt/image/author/dates.
- Useful taxonomies such as merchant, offer type, and/or deal category only if each is maintainable and not empty.
- ACF fields, with field type, key, validation, instructions, and frontend behavior documented:
  - Merchant/brand name
  - Offer title and short summary
  - Offer type: Coupon code, Automatic discount, Free trial, Special pricing, Other
  - Coupon code (optional; never fabricate)
  - Destination URL
  - Affiliate URL and relationship status, if distinct and verified
  - Discount/value text (optional and source-backed)
  - Valid-from date (optional)
  - Expiry date (optional)
  - Verification status: Unverified, Verified, Expired, Terms changed, Withdrawn
  - Last checked date
  - Source/terms URL
  - Eligibility and exclusions
  - Terms summary
  - Featured/pinned status only if genuinely needed
  - Related comparison/post
- Validate URLs and dates. Expired offers must not look active. If an expiry date is unknown, do not invent one or display a false “ending soon” message.
- Add editor-side help text explaining exactly how to verify and expire offers. If safe automatic expiry cannot be implemented and tested, provide a documented manual workflow rather than claiming automation.
- Never publish a coupon code, percentage, free trial, pricing claim, or “exclusive” claim unless verified against a reliable source. If the offer cannot be verified, create it as a **Draft / Needs verification** record and do not display it as an active deal.

### D. ACF and field portability

- Prefer ACF Free for fields if available in the target. Confirm the actual installed version and supported field types.
- Register CPTs/taxonomies in a small `ReadMeHub Core` functionality plugin where possible, so content survives theme changes.
- Include ACF field groups in a portable form supported by the target (ACF Local JSON and/or documented PHP registration when appropriate). Do not invent field keys or pretend fields exist until the package includes them.
- Document each field's source, type, return format, required/optional rule, default/fallback, empty state, editor instructions, validation, output escaping/sanitization, stale-state behavior, and template usage.
- If ACF is unavailable, do not silently switch to an incompatible paid feature; provide the install requirement and a documented native fallback.
- Test that an editor can create a record, save it, preview it, publish it, edit it later, and see the correct front-end output.

## 6. Complete content: required launch seed

Deliver complete, readable content—not lorem ipsum, filler paragraphs, “coming soon,” or empty archive cards.

Minimum seed inventory:
- **3 complete blog posts** (minimum 2–3 means deliver 3), each with useful original prose, descriptive headings, summary/excerpt, category, featured image or suitable licensed/locally stored visual, relevant internal links, author/date handled truthfully, and sources for factual/current claims.
- **At least 1 complete comparison record** with full comparison-page content and structured fields populated. Prefer 2 comparisons if time and evidence allow.
- **At least 2 deal/coupon records** in the dashboard, with complete fields and terms/source notes. At least one can be shown publicly only if the offer is actually verified and currently valid. If no reliable active offer can be verified, keep the records in Draft/Needs verification and create a useful, honest “No verified offers currently available” archive state rather than fake a deal.
- **At least 3 populated categories** that each have meaningful description and real post assignment, unless the launch content strategy explicitly justifies fewer.
- About, Methodology, Editorial Standards, Affiliate Disclosure, Contact/Report Error, Privacy draft, Terms draft, and 404 content.
- Homepage sections should use real seeded posts/comparisons/deals, and every CTA must point to a working page or meaningful action.
- Include a content inventory table in the handoff with title, content type, URL/slug, publication status, category, sources, evidence/verification status, and any follow-up required.

### Editorial integrity and research

Research current product, plan, coupon, pricing, eligibility, and terms facts from primary/official sources wherever possible. Store source URLs and the date checked in the appropriate admin fields or editorial source notes. Distinguish source-verified facts from editorial opinion. Never claim ReadMeHub purchased subscriptions, performed hands-on tests, stress-tested APIs, independently benchmarked products, interviewed customers, or gathered survey data unless those actions actually occurred and evidence is supplied.

The ZIP's current HTML contains some strong trust/test claims, review ratings, review counts, pricing, dated statements, and social/company details. Treat these as **unverified sample content**, not automatically true facts. Keep the design, not unsubstantiated claims. Remove or rewrite unsupported ratings, review counts, testimonials, author credentials, performance claims, subscriber/user counts, prices, test results, “independent authority” statements, and social links. If a page requires evidence that does not exist, state the limitation clearly and do not simulate evidence. Do not copy large blocks of competitor/product marketing text; write original editorial copy.

For the three blog posts, choose a coherent launch niche supported by the supplied spec and reference. If the owner has not selected a niche, choose a provisional content cluster, label the decision **PROVISIONAL**, justify it in the handoff, and keep it easy to change. Do not build dozens of unrelated category hubs.

## 7. Header and footer: special acceptance requirement

The header and footer must visually match the ZIP as closely as possible. Treat this as a high-priority acceptance criterion, not a suggestion.

For the header:
- Inspect the exact desktop and mobile markup, content, order, dimensions, spacing, colors, typography, borders, icons, dropdowns, theme toggle, search, and CTA.
- Rebuild as an editable Elementor-compatible reusable header if the verified free stack supports it.
- If global header template conditions are unavailable in the installed Free/Xpro configuration, implement a small theme/child-theme header template with equivalent appearance and accessible behavior. Do not fake a global header by manually duplicating inconsistent versions on every page unless there is no maintainable alternative.
- Verify dropdown keyboard behavior, focus visibility, Escape/close behavior where applicable, menu state, mobile toggle labels/expanded state, and no overflow.
- Do not leave dead menu items. Hide or disable routes not yet implemented.

For the footer:
- Match the reference structure, brand block, text width, link columns, icon/social area, background, spacing, and responsive stacking.
- Remove unverified claims in the footer copy while preserving its design anatomy.
- Every published link must work. Do not invent social profile URLs; use only confirmed links, or omit the icon/link while preserving layout balance.

Capture side-by-side screenshots of the ZIP reference and rebuilt pages at comparable desktop and mobile viewports. Document material differences and why they remain.

## 8. Elementor build rules

- Build with WordPress + Elementor Free first, and use Xpro only after verifying that a required widget/function is actually available in its free tier.
- Use native Elementor containers and native widgets for headings, text, buttons, images, icons, lists, and cards wherever practical.
- Use actual WordPress posts/CPT records for blog, category, comparison, and deals archives; do not hard-code article grids that editors cannot maintain.
- Prefer real templates/query mechanisms available in the installed stack. If Elementor Free cannot dynamically render a required archive or ACF field, choose a tested WordPress/theme template or a verified free alternative and document it.
- Do not use Elementor Pro-only widgets, paid Xpro widgets, JetEngine/Crocoblock, or paid dynamic-content plugins unless the owner explicitly approves a new dependency.
- No single giant HTML widget for an entire page, no iframe containing the reference site, no static screenshot-as-site, no giant shortcode to hide the build, and no minified unreadable code dump.
- Scoped CSS is allowed for visual parity, dark mode, or unsupported layout details. Prefix custom selectors/variables with `rmh-` / `--rmh-`, document them, and avoid broad selectors that break Elementor or WordPress admin.
- Use JavaScript only for necessary behavior unavailable in native/theme controls; keep it minimal, accessible, and resilient when JS fails.
- Do not claim a section is drag-and-drop editable unless it has actually been opened and tested in the WordPress editor.
- Preserve reusable component anatomy and global design tokens; instance-specific content must remain independently editable.

## 9. Dark mode, responsive behavior, and accessibility

Implement the light/dark appearance from the reference if present. Use semantic token overrides rather than indiscriminate color inversion. Theme control must be a real accessible control with a clear label/state, keyboard operation, focus style, and stable behavior. Test the saved preference and system preference only if implemented. Do not invert product images or logos.

Test:
- Desktop (e.g. 1440px and 1280px)
- Tablet (e.g. 1024px and 768px)
- Mobile (e.g. 390px and 360px)
- One intermediate width where layout transitions
- 200% browser zoom/reflow
- Keyboard-only navigation
- Focus visibility, dropdown/menu open/close, search, pagination, forms, theme toggle, coupon code copy if implemented, and all CTA links
- No horizontal page overflow, no clipped headings/buttons, no overlapping cards, and no unreadable table/card comparison
- Semantic heading hierarchy, one meaningful H1 per page, landmarks, meaningful alt text, decorative-image empty alt, accessible labels and error messages, non-color-only status, reduced-motion preference, contrast checks for both themes.

Do not claim WCAG compliance based only on visual inspection. Record actual checks, tools, failures, and fixes.

## 10. SEO, performance, security, and structured data

- Clean permalinks and canonical URLs; accurate titles/meta descriptions; meaningful internal links; proper archive pagination; no indexable thin/empty pages.
- Add schema only where content genuinely qualifies (e.g. Article, BreadcrumbList, Organization only with accurate details). Do not add fabricated Review/AggregateRating/Product/Offer schema or markup for ratings/prices/discounts that are absent or unverified.
- Optimize images locally as appropriate (WebP/AVIF if supported), include dimensions and alt text, avoid unnecessary external CDN scripts, avoid loading all icon libraries if not needed, and keep CSS/JS lean.
- Use WordPress APIs and escaping/sanitization/validation for custom functionality; check capability/nonce where custom admin actions or forms require it. Do not put API keys or secrets into theme/plugin files or public HTML.
- No fake analytics, fake social proof, or fabricated performance metrics. Performance claims require a real test result with date, environment, and method.

## 11. Transfer-ready deliverable

Provide an installable, logically organized package. Recommended structure:

- `readmehub-core/` — custom functionality plugin for CPTs/taxonomies and any necessary non-visual behavior.
- `readmehub-theme/` or `readmehub-child-theme/` — only if required; minimal, update-safe, Elementor-compatible theme integration.
- `acf-json/` and/or documented ACF field-group registration.
- Elementor templates/kit/export only where the actual installed Elementor version supports reliable export/import; never label handcrafted JSON as importable without testing.
- `assets/` — local logos/icons/images/fonts only when licensing permits, with a manifest and source/license notes.
- `README-INSTALL.md` — exact clean-WordPress installation order, required plugins/versions, import steps, permalink refresh, menu assignment, homepage/posts page setup, global settings, field setup, media import, and troubleshooting.
- `CONTENT-INVENTORY.md` — seed content, source notes, statuses, slugs, categories.
- `DESIGN-TOKENS.md` — extracted ZIP tokens and where each is implemented.
- `FIELD-DICTIONARY.md` — CPTs, taxonomies, ACF fields/types/keys, validations, fallbacks and display locations.
- `QA-REPORT.md` — actual test evidence and failures.
- `KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md` — unresolved gaps, dependency/license limits, owner decisions.
- `CHANGELOG.md` — what was implemented and changed.

Package so it can be installed on a fresh WordPress environment without depending on your temporary workspace. Do not include secrets, cache files, debug logs with private data, or unrelated files. If the actual Elementor Kit/template export cannot be generated from the installed target, say so and provide the tested alternative.

## 12. Execution phases — do not skip

### Phase 1: Input and environment audit
Read both complete inputs. Inspect every section of the HTML and Markdown specification. Inventory the actual assets, links, sections, interactions, claims, and existing environment. Report blockers, then continue with all work that does not depend on unavailable access.

### Phase 2: Reference design extraction
Create a source-grounded design token and component map. Record header/footer exact anatomy and all page sections. Capture reference screenshots if possible. Do not begin with a generic template.

### Phase 3: Architecture and content model
Define CPTs, taxonomies, field groups, archive/single relationships, URLs, content ownership, verification states, and editor workflow. Verify field types and query/template capability before implementation.

### Phase 4: Representative component proof
Build header, footer, a blog card, comparison card, deal card, and a representative single-content layout. Confirm editability, token propagation, mobile behavior, dark mode, and admin editing before replicating patterns.

### Phase 5: Full site implementation
Build all required page families and populate the launch seed content. Implement real WordPress archive/query behavior and custom fields. Use verified free dependencies only.

### Phase 6: Editorial and offer verification
Verify all current facts, product terms, prices, coupon codes, expiry dates, destinations, author details, and claims. Store source and last-checked details. Unverified offers remain Draft/Needs verification and cannot be shown as active.

### Phase 7: QA and correction
Run responsive, accessibility, interaction, content, SEO, security, performance, admin-editing, and migration tests. Compare screenshots with the ZIP. Fix all critical and high-severity issues; retest.

### Phase 8: Independent reviewer passes
Run the 16 roles listed above as real review passes. Produce issue IDs, severity, evidence, resolution, and residual risk. Do not mark a problem fixed without retesting.

### Phase 9: Packaging and handoff
Create and verify the package. Test installation on a clean WordPress environment if possible. If not possible, mark that test **NOT RUN** and give exact manual verification steps. Provide a concise summary of what is complete, what is not, and what the owner must decide.

## 13. Acceptance criteria

The job is complete only when all applicable items below are demonstrably true:

- [ ] ZIP and Markdown spec fully inspected.
- [ ] Reference visual identity is retained; token values documented.
- [ ] Header and footer match the reference closely, with screenshots and difference notes.
- [ ] Home, blog index, single post, category archive, comparison index/single, deals index/single, search, about, methodology, disclosure, contact/report error, legal drafts, and 404 are implemented.
- [ ] Minimum 3 complete blog posts exist and render correctly.
- [ ] Minimum 1 complete comparison record exists and renders correctly.
- [ ] Minimum 2 deal/coupon records exist in the admin; only verified current offers appear publicly active.
- [ ] Custom post types/taxonomies and ACF fields are visible and usable in WordPress admin.
- [ ] A non-developer can create/edit/preview/publish a post, comparison, and deal without code.
- [ ] Archives pull real records; no hard-coded fake cards or broken links.
- [ ] All major sections are editable; no page-sized HTML widget or flattened screenshot.
- [ ] Responsive, dark-mode, keyboard, forms, menu, search, and coupon/deal states are tested.
- [ ] Unsupported ratings, reviews, test claims, pricing, testimonials, credentials, and social links are removed or verified.
- [ ] SEO/schema does not introduce unsupported claims.
- [ ] Package includes installation guide, field dictionary, content inventory, design tokens, QA report, known limitations, and changelog.
- [ ] Clean-install/import test has an evidence-based status.
- [ ] No critical/high severity QA issue remains unresolved without explicit disclosure.

## 14. Required final report format

Return these sections in the final handoff:

1. **Executive summary** — what was actually built and the current status.
2. **Source audit** — what the ZIP contains and how the specification was applied.
3. **Environment/dependency inventory** — exact versions and free/pro status confirmed.
4. **Design-system extraction** — actual values, source locations, and implementation destinations.
5. **Header/footer parity report** — desktop/mobile screenshots, match notes, differences.
6. **Page/template inventory** — each required page, route, source/template mechanism, and status.
7. **Content inventory** — 3+ posts, comparisons, deals/coupons, categories, sources, publication/verification state.
8. **Admin content model** — CPTs, taxonomies, ACF field groups, field types/keys, validations, fallback behavior.
9. **Elementor editability report** — what uses native widgets, what uses Xpro, what uses theme templates/custom code, and why.
10. **QA matrix** — test, environment, result, evidence, issue ID, retest result.
11. **Installation and migration guide** — exact steps for a clean WordPress install and target deployment.
12. **Known limitations and owner decisions** — explicit, actionable, no vague “may need work.”
13. **Final file manifest** — each package file and purpose, plus package validation status.

Use explicit statuses: `PASS`, `FAIL`, `PARTIAL`, `BLOCKED`, `NOT RUN`, `NEEDS OWNER DECISION`. Never call a feature complete simply because code was generated. Never claim a site is live, importable, responsive, accessible, secure, or tested unless the corresponding verification was actually performed.

## Final instruction

Begin now by inspecting both supplied files completely. Then execute the phases in order and produce the working, editable, transfer-ready ReadMeHub website. Do not stop after producing another plan or prompt. Do not ask the owner to repeat information already contained in the files. If an essential environment access or decision is missing, identify it precisely, complete all independent work, and deliver the strongest verifiable package possible without fabricating success.

### Source file 30: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/02_Master_Prompts/ReadMeHub_2026_Claude_Final_Synthesis_Prompt.md`

# READMEHUB 2026 — FINAL MULTI-SOURCE SYNTHESIS PROMPT

## Role

You are the FINAL STRATEGIC SYNTHESIS ARCHITECT for ReadMeHub.

You have been given independent research reports from multiple AI research systems.

Do NOT summarize them.

Your job is to reconcile evidence, detect conflicts, verify important claims, challenge assumptions, and produce one final evidence-based ReadMeHub strategy.

## Source Independence

Treat each supplied report as an independent input.

Do not assume repeated claims are independently confirmed if they originate from the same underlying source.

Prioritize primary evidence over repeated secondary claims.

Do not preserve a previous idea merely because it appears in multiple reports.

## 1. Source Audit

For every material strategic conclusion identify source, date, primary/secondary status, evidence strength, agreement/disagreement, and confidence.

## 2. Conflict Detection

Create:
CLAIM → REPORT A → REPORT B → REPORT C → REPORT D → PRIMARY-SOURCE CHECK → CONCLUSION → CONFIDENCE

Do not average conflicting claims. Investigate why they conflict.

## 3. Fact / Estimate / Inference

Classify important conclusions as:
FACT / OBSERVATION / ESTIMATE / INFERENCE / INDUSTRY OPINION / UNKNOWN

Never convert an estimate into a fact.

## 4. Competitor Reconciliation

Merge competitor datasets, remove duplicates, and identify repeated competitors, one-report-only competitors, missed competitor classes, small publishers, mid-size publishers, large publishers, direct competitors, adjacent competitors, review publishers, comparison publishers, and deals publishers.

Determine the final deep competitor set based on evidence, not arbitrary ranking.

## 5. Re-evaluate the 13 Categories

Re-evaluate:
1. Software & SaaS
2. Artificial Intelligence
3. Health & Wellness
4. Technology & Electronics
5. Entertainment & Media
6. Dating & Relationships
7. Finance & Money Tools
8. Education & Career
9. Home & Lifestyle
10. Travel & Experiences
11. Shopping & Consumer Products
12. Business Services
13. Deals, Coupons & Offers

Classify:
LAUNCH / PHASE 2 / PHASE 3 / EXPERIMENT / DEPRIORITIZE / DO NOT PURSUE

Explain decisions using evidence.

## 6. Business Model Challenge

Try to disprove the ReadMeHub model.

Test organic search dependency, AI-search dependency, zero-click, AdSense economics, affiliate economics, CPA, advertiser demand, content cost, maintenance, authority, category dilution, competition, compliance, and operational complexity.

## 7. Final Business Model

Determine the evidence-supported model.

Potential layers include SEO, AI-search visibility, content, reviews, comparisons, recommendations, decision tools, deals, affiliate, CPA, display advertising, newsletter, direct partnerships, original research, and future data/tools.

Only include layers supported by evidence.

## 8. Final Content Strategy

Determine what to publish, what not to publish, what to test, what requires first-hand evidence, what can be AI-assisted, what requires human review, what should be updated, and what should be consolidated.

## 9. Final Website Architecture

Create the information architecture, category architecture, URL architecture, navigation, content hierarchy, entity model, page types, internal linking, and UX priorities.

Respect WordPress + Hello Elementor + Elementor + ACF + lightweight implementation + no custom theme + no unnecessary plugins/addons.

## 10. Final Monetization

Build an evidence-based revenue model for AdSense/display, affiliate, CPA, lead generation, newsletter, direct advertising, sponsorship, partnerships, and future revenue.

Model realistic scenarios; do not guarantee outcomes.

## 11. Final AI Operating System

Create:
Research → SERP → Keywords → Brief → Sources → Content → Fact Check → Evidence Check → SEO → Images → Schema → QA → Compliance → Human Approval → Publish → Monitor → Update.

Define agents, permissions, validation, escalation, human checkpoints, logging, and rollback.

## 12. Final 0→Traffic→Revenue Roadmap

Produce:
0–30 days
31–60 days
61–90 days
3–6 months
6–12 months

Use measurable milestones and exit criteria. Do not promise timelines.

## 13. $500–$1,000 AdSense Analysis

Evaluate the stated target.

Model required traffic, pageviews, RPM scenarios, geography, traffic mix, seasonality, content inventory, and uncertainty.

Also model affiliate/CPA/direct partnership revenue separately.

## 14. Final Failure Analysis

Identify 10–20 serious failure modes.

For each:
RISK / CAUSE / EARLY SIGNAL / MITIGATION / TRIGGER / ACTION

## 15. Final Master Strategy

Answer:
WHAT / WHY / WHO / WHERE / WHEN / HOW / WITH WHAT / AT WHAT PRIORITY / WITH WHAT KPI / WITH WHAT RISK

## Final Deliverables

1. Executive Summary
2. Evidence Audit
3. Research Conflicts
4. Final Competitor Landscape
5. Final 13-Category Strategy
6. Final Search/SERP Strategy
7. Final AI-Search Strategy
8. Final Content Strategy
9. Final Content Moat
10. Final Information Architecture
11. Final Technical Architecture
12. Final Monetization
13. Final AdSense/Affiliate/Compliance Framework
14. Final Advertiser Strategy
15. Final Newsletter Strategy
16. Final Deals Strategy
17. Final AI Operating System
18. Final Traffic Acquisition
19. 90-Day Execution Plan
20. 6–12 Month Roadmap
21. Revenue Scenarios
22. KPI Dashboard
23. Risk Register
24. Failure/Kill-Switch Framework
25. FINAL READMEHUB MASTER STRATEGY
26. FINAL DO THIS / DO NOT DO THIS CHECKLIST

End with:

"THE FINAL STRATEGY I WOULD IMPLEMENT IF I WERE RESPONSIBLE FOR THE PROJECT'S TIME, MONEY AND REPUTATION"

This must be evidence-based, not motivational.

If evidence supports changing the proposed model, change it.

If evidence is insufficient, say so.

If multiple strategies are viable under different conditions, explain the conditions rather than selecting a winner without evidence.

### Source file 31: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/02_Master_Prompts/ReadMeHub_2026_Masterstroke_Research_Prompt.md`

# READMEHUB 2026 — ZERO-BASED MASTERSTROKE RESEARCH PROMPT

## Purpose

Conduct a complete, evidence-based investigation for building ReadMeHub from zero into a sustainable digital publisher, recommendation, discovery, decision-support, affiliate, advertising, and commerce-oriented platform.

Do not treat ReadMeHub as merely an SEO blog.

Do not assume the proposed business model or 13-category structure is correct. Determine what the 2026 market actually supports.

## Critical Zero-Based Rule

START FROM THE 2026 MARKET.

Do not use previous ReadMeHub strategy, competitor lists, keyword lists, category priorities, monetization assumptions, or strategic conclusions as evidence.

If old ReadMeHub materials are supplied later, they may only be used AFTER fresh research for a separate comparison/audit. They must not anchor the initial research.

If evidence contradicts the owner's idea, say so clearly.

If evidence is insufficient, say INSUFFICIENT EVIDENCE.

Never manufacture certainty.

## Mission

Determine the most realistic path:

ZERO → MARKET VALIDATION → POSITIONING → ARCHITECTURE → WEBSITE BUILD → CONTENT → INDEXATION → FIRST VISITORS → RETURNING USERS → NEWSLETTER → AFFILIATE/CPA CONVERSIONS → ADVERTISING → DIRECT PARTNERSHIPS → REPEATABLE REVENUE → SCALING → AI-ASSISTED OPERATIONS.

Do not guarantee rankings, traffic, AdSense approval, affiliate approval, revenue, advertiser partnerships, or AI citations.

---

# THE 15 MASTER RESEARCH SYSTEMS

## 1. Market & Business Viability Intelligence

Research the 2026 market for digital publishing, consumer recommendations, affiliate publishing, display advertising, commerce content, review/comparison publishing, deals/coupon publishing, AI-assisted publishing, search-driven publishing, newsletters, CPA/lead generation, and direct publisher partnerships.

Evaluate demand, competition, barriers to entry, capital requirements, authority requirements, content requirements, freshness requirements, monetization maturity, advertiser demand, affiliate demand, AI-search risk, zero-click risk, platform dependency, compliance burden, and operational complexity.

Determine what should be built now, later, tested, changed, or rejected.

## 2. Competitor & Publisher Intelligence

Build a discovery pool of approximately 100–150+ relevant publisher/site/business candidates where feasible.

Classify direct competitors, broad publishers, affiliate publishers, review publishers, comparison publishers, deals/coupon publishers, consumer recommendation publishers, technology publishers, software/SaaS publishers, vertical publishers, SEO-first publishers, small publishers, mid-size publishers, large publishers, and discovery/recommendation platforms.

Select approximately 30–40 strategically relevant entities for deep analysis, including approximately 5–10 closest structural/direct competitors and 10–15 strategic benchmarks. Do not force numbers if a category genuinely has fewer relevant entities.

Discover competitors using Google Search/SERPs, AI search, category-specific searches, publisher discovery, reputable industry sources, SEO/traffic intelligence where accessible, affiliate/publisher ecosystems, and small/mid-size publisher discovery.

Do not make individual vendor blogs the primary competitor set.

For each deep competitor analyze audience, geography, categories, content model, content types, SEO/search strategy, keyword strategy, SERP strategy, AI-search visibility, original research, testing/review methodology, editorial standards, authorship, freshness/update frequency, information architecture, UX, navigation, internal linking, technical SEO, performance, accessibility, newsletter, distribution, affiliate model, advertising, CPA/lead generation, direct partnerships, sponsorship, deals, commercial pages, trust signals, disclosures, strengths, weaknesses, content gaps, keyword gaps, SERP gaps, UX gaps, monetization gaps, AI-search gaps, and operational weaknesses.

Study small → growing → established publisher paths where evidence exists.

## 3. Search, SERP & Keyword Intelligence

Research search intent, SERP composition, organic results, AI Overviews, AI Mode, featured snippets, People Also Ask, shopping results, ads, Reddit/forums, YouTube/video results, comparison sites, publishers, vendors, local results where relevant, SERP volatility, commercial intent, freshness requirements, ranking difficulty, and authority requirements.

Identify head terms, mid-tail, long-tail, problem queries, use-case queries, comparison, alternatives, best, pricing, deal, coupon, review, decision, and how-to queries.

Build keyword → intent → page-type mapping.

Every meaningful intent should have one primary URL owner unless a documented reason exists for multiple pages.

Identify cannibalization, overlap, thin variants, consolidation opportunities, and pages that should not exist.

## 4. AI Search & Zero-Click Survival

Research Google AI Overviews, Google AI Mode, AI answer engines, citation behavior, source selection, entity recognition, zero-click behavior, AI referrals, and click-through opportunities.

Determine which content AI can easily answer, which content still creates reasons to click, what can become AI-answer-resistant, what original information could earn citation value, and what interactive functionality can create clicks.

Evaluate original research, unique datasets, comparisons, testing evidence, screenshots, decision frameworks, calculators, tools, databases, price/deal verification, and structured entity data.

Do not claim secret AI SEO tricks.

## 5. Content Moat & First-Hand Evidence

Determine what ReadMeHub can produce that generic AI content cannot easily reproduce.

Research hands-on testing, product/software testing, screenshots, workflows, setup guides, price verification, feature verification, experiments, benchmarks, original comparisons, original research, change logs, updates, and decision frameworks.

Classify content by evidence level:
1. Commodity information
2. Synthesized information
3. Expert analysis
4. Verified evidence
5. Original research/testing/data
6. Proprietary tools/datasets/decision systems

Never claim testing, use, measurement, verification, purchase, contact, or experience without evidence.

## 6. Category & Information Architecture

Proposed categories:
1. Software & SaaS
2. Artificial Intelligence
3. Health & Wellness
4. Technology & Electronics
5. Entertainment & Media
6. Dating & Relationships
7. Finance & Money Tools
8. Education & Career
9. Home & Lifestyle
10. Travel & Experiences
11. Shopping & Consumer Products
12. Business Services
13. Deals, Coupons & Offers

Do not assume equal priority.

For each category evaluate demand, competition, commercial intent, affiliate opportunity, CPA opportunity, advertising opportunity, advertiser opportunity, content difficulty, authority difficulty, freshness, compliance, AI-search risk, zero-click risk, content cost, monetization potential, launch suitability, expansion suitability, and original evidence potential.

Classify categories as Launch, Phase 2, Phase 3, Experiment, Deprioritize, or Do not pursue.

Design Category → Subcategory → Topic → Entity → Use Case → Intent → Content Type → URL.

Cover taxonomy, breadcrumbs, category hubs, subcategory hubs, mega menu, mobile navigation, search, filters, archives, internal linking, entity relationships, and related content.

## 7. Content Strategy & Editorial Authority

Evaluate reviews, comparisons, alternatives, best-of, buying guides, how-to, explainers, research, product/entity pages, software/entity pages, brand pages, category pages, use-case pages, problem/solution pages, deals, coupons, offers, updates, news where justified, tools, calculators, databases, and resources.

For each content type determine demand, intent, monetization, difficulty, evidence requirement, update frequency, AI-search vulnerability, production cost, and launch status.

Classify BUILD NOW / BUILD LATER / EXPERIMENT / DO NOT BUILD.

## 8. Content Economics & Production Capacity

Determine whether the content strategy is operationally feasible for a small/new publisher.

Estimate and clearly label estimates for research, writing, editing, fact checking, images, SEO, publishing, maintenance, testing, update frequency, and lifespan.

Model production cost, maintenance cost, content ROI, commercial value, freshness cost, and opportunity cost.

Determine sustainable publishing velocity and a realistic initial content inventory.

Do not recommend mass publishing merely because AI makes drafting faster.

## 9. SEO, Technical SEO, Entity & Structured Data

Research crawlability, indexation, sitemap, robots, canonical, redirects, pagination, duplicate content, orphan pages, internal links, image SEO, JavaScript rendering, performance, Core Web Vitals, mobile usability, accessibility, security, structured data, entity architecture, author architecture, breadcrumbs, organization, article, product where appropriate, and review where appropriate.

Do not use schema for manipulation. Structured data must represent visible page content and follow current guidance.

## 10. Monetization & Revenue Architecture

Research AdSense/display, affiliate, CPA, lead generation, direct advertising, sponsorship, newsletter sponsorship, commercial partnerships, deal partnerships, tools, and future premium/data products where justified.

Determine suitable monetization mix by category.

Build: TRAFFIC → ENGAGEMENT → DECISION SUPPORT → COMMERCIAL INTENT → AFFILIATE/CPA/LEAD → DISPLAY → NEWSLETTER → DIRECT PARTNERSHIPS → FUTURE REVENUE.

## 11. AdSense, Affiliate, Legal & Compliance

Research current 2026 Google Publisher Policies, Google Search spam policies, scaled content abuse, thin affiliation, replicated content, low-value content, AI-assisted content, affiliate disclosures, sponsored content, privacy, cookies, consent, GDPR, UK considerations, US considerations, Canada considerations, Australia considerations, financial content risks, health content risks, dating safety, consumer protection, corrections, and editorial independence.

Distinguish FACT / REGULATORY REQUIREMENT / PLATFORM POLICY / BEST PRACTICE / RISK / PROFESSIONAL-ADVICE REQUIRED.

## 12. Advertiser & Partnership Readiness

Research what makes publishers attractive to affiliate managers, brands, advertisers, SaaS companies, product companies, agencies, CPA networks, and direct partners.

Evaluate publisher credibility, audience quality, traffic quality, editorial standards, media kit, advertiser page, partnership page, audience data, newsletter, commercial intent, original research, brand safety, and transparency.

Evaluate whether these assets are needed: Advertise, Partner With Us, Media Kit, Submit a Product, Submit a Deal, Affiliate Disclosure, Editorial Policy, Methodology, Testing Policy, Corrections, Report an Error, Update Request, Contact.

Do not create pages just because they sound professional.

## 13. AI Publisher Operating System

Design an AI-assisted workflow:

Research → Competitor Research → SERP Research → Keyword Discovery → Opportunity Scoring → Topic Selection → Brief → Source Collection → Draft → Fact Check → Original Value Check → SEO → Image → Schema → Editorial QA → Compliance QA → Human Approval → Publish → Index Monitoring → Performance Monitoring → Update Detection → Refresh.

For every agent define purpose, inputs, outputs, tools, permissions, forbidden actions, validation, escalation, human approval, logging, rollback, and error handling.

Include hallucination firewall, source hierarchy, citation verification, claim verification, duplicate detection, outdated-information detection, affiliate-link verification, price verification, policy verification, schema validation, editorial review, and sensitive-topic review.

Do not use AI detector scores as proof of quality.

## 14. Traffic Acquisition & Growth

Research Google organic, AI-search visibility, newsletter, direct, referral, social, video, Pinterest where relevant, digital PR, original research, partnerships, community participation, email, and commercial partnerships.

Do not recommend spam, fake traffic, fake engagement, manipulative links, fake reviews, or keyword stuffing.

Create measurable growth stages: 0 → first impression → first click → 100 visitors → 1,000 → 10,000 → returning users → newsletter → commercial conversions.

No guaranteed timeline.

## 15. Measurement, Failure, Pivot & Kill-Switch

Track indexed URLs, impressions, clicks, CTR, rankings, organic users, returning users, branded searches, newsletter, affiliate clicks, conversions, EPC where available, revenue/page, AdSense RPM, page RPM, advertiser leads, content ROI, update ROI, AI-search visibility, referral traffic, category performance, and content decay.

Define CONTINUE / OPTIMIZE / CONSOLIDATE / PAUSE / PIVOT / KILL with measurable triggers.

---

# REQUIRED 13-CATEGORY OPPORTUNITY MATRIX

For every category include market demand, search demand, competition, authority difficulty, commercial intent, affiliate availability, CPA, advertising, direct advertiser potential, content cost, freshness cost, AI-search risk, zero-click risk, compliance risk, trust requirement, original evidence potential, launch suitability, expansion suitability, recommended content types, recommended monetization, major competitors, opportunity gaps, and risks.

# CONTENT GAP ANALYSIS

Identify missing topics, underserved intents, unanswered questions, weak comparisons, outdated content, poor UX, weak decision support, missing tools, missing datasets, missing evidence, missing regional coverage, missing pricing context, weak transparency, weak freshness systems, and weak commercial journeys.

Separate CONTENT GAP / KEYWORD GAP / SERP GAP / UX GAP / PRODUCT GAP / TRUST GAP / MONETIZATION GAP / AI-SEARCH GAP.

# FRESHNESS SYSTEM

Classify content as evergreen, annual, quarterly, monthly, continuously monitored, or event-driven.

Track price, availability, features, affiliate status, coupon status, offers, screenshots, policies, statistics, and sources where relevant.

Use Created / Last Verified / Last Updated / Next Review.

# WEBSITE & UX

Research optimal publisher architecture including homepage, category hubs, subcategory hubs, search, mega menu, mobile navigation, filters, comparison UX, recommendation UX, article UX, product/software entity pages, deals UX, newsletter UX, trust UX, author UX, methodology UX, and advertiser UX.

The website must not be an attractive but strategically empty blog.

# TECHNICAL CONSTRAINTS

Implementation must use WordPress + Hello Elementor + Elementor + ACF + lightweight custom CSS/JS where necessary.

DO NOT replace Hello Elementor.
DO NOT create/activate a custom theme.
DO NOT replace Elementor.
DO NOT introduce unnecessary Elementor addons.
DO NOT introduce plugin bloat.

Prioritize performance, accessibility, responsive design, security, maintainability, structured content, and scalability.

# AI + HUMAN GOVERNANCE

Create permission levels:
LEVEL 0 — Research only
LEVEL 1 — Drafting
LEVEL 2 — Suggested changes
LEVEL 3 — Low-risk automation
LEVEL 4 — Human approval required
LEVEL 5 — Never automated

Sensitive areas require human review, including health, finance, legal/compliance, dating safety, product safety, pricing, affiliate disclosures, advertiser claims, and high-risk claims.

Create change logging and rollback.

# REVENUE ECONOMICS

The owner has a target of approximately $500–$1,000/month in AdSense revenue beginning around month 3.

DO NOT validate this automatically.

Model required traffic, pageviews, RPM scenarios, geography, traffic mix, seasonality, content inventory, and uncertainty.

Provide CONSERVATIVE / BASE / AGGRESSIVE / FAILURE scenarios.

Also model affiliate/CPA/direct partnership revenue separately.

# DARK-SIDE REALITY CHECK

Try to DISPROVE the model.

Investigate Google dependency, AI-search traffic loss, zero-click, affiliate saturation, AdSense economics, low RPM, low conversion, advertiser rejection, affiliate rejection, category dilution, authority dilution, content maintenance, AI-content saturation, competitor dominance, indexing limitations, content decay, compliance risk, health/finance sensitivity, operational complexity, third-party platform dependency, weak differentiation, newsletter difficulty, and advertiser acquisition difficulty.

Answer:
"What are the most realistic ways this project can fail?"
"What early evidence would tell us to change course?"

# SMALL-PUBLISHER REALITY

Do not build around giant publishers' resources.

Identify small and mid-sized publishers and study initial niche, early content, early monetization, early traffic, authority building, expansion, and operations.

# SOURCE & EVIDENCE PROTOCOL

Prefer:

TIER 1: official Google, government/regulator, platform documentation, official company/publisher disclosures, primary research, official reports.

TIER 2: reputable industry research, established journalism, credible research organizations, reputable analytics.

TIER 3: expert analysis, community, Reddit/forums, commentary.

For important claims provide CLAIM / SOURCE / DATE / EVIDENCE / CONFIDENCE / INTERPRETATION.

Distinguish FACT / OBSERVATION / ESTIMATE / INFERENCE / INDUSTRY OPINION / UNKNOWN.

Never fabricate numbers.

# RESEARCH QUALITY CONTROL

Perform a second-pass audit:
1. What evidence is missing?
2. Which conclusions depend on weak evidence?
3. Which sources conflict?
4. Which assumptions were introduced?
5. Which competitor classes are missing?
6. Which small publishers were missed?
7. Which SERPs were insufficiently researched?
8. Which monetization assumptions are weak?
9. Which compliance questions need professional verification?
10. Which recommendations rely on opinion?
11. What would change the recommendation?
12. What did the research fail to prove?

# STRATEGIC DECISION FRAMEWORK

Determine:
WHAT TO BUILD NOW
WHAT TO BUILD LATER
WHAT TO TEST
WHAT NOT TO BUILD
WHAT TO AUTOMATE
WHAT MUST REMAIN HUMAN
WHAT TO MEASURE
WHAT TO STOP
WHAT TO CHANGE
WHAT TO INVEST IN
WHAT NOT TO SPEND ON

# 0→REVENUE ROADMAP

Cover:
Validation → Market selection → Competitor intelligence → Category selection → Search/SERP mapping → IA → Positioning → UX/UI → WordPress/Elementor/ACF architecture → Technical SEO → Trust/compliance → Initial content → Launch → Indexation → First impressions/clicks → Organic growth → Affiliate conversion → Advertising readiness → Newsletter growth → Advertiser outreach → Partnerships → Scaling → AI operations → Optimization → Revenue diversification.

For every phase specify objective, prerequisites, tasks, output, dependencies, tools, KPI, risks, and exit criteria.

# 90-DAY PLAN

Break down Day 0–7, Day 8–14, Day 15–30, Day 31–60, Day 61–90.

Do not assume hundreds of articles are required.

# WEBSITE PAGE STRATEGY

Evaluate homepage, category pages, subcategory pages, reviews, comparisons, alternatives, best-of, buying guides, product/entity pages, software/entity pages, deals, coupons, tools, research, resources, updates, methodology, editorial policy, testing policy, authors, advertise, partnerships, media kit, submit product, submit deal, report error, update request, about, contact, privacy, terms, affiliate disclosure, newsletter.

Classify REQUIRED / RECOMMENDED / OPTIONAL / FUTURE / UNNECESSARY.

# NEWSLETTER

Newsletter collection is required from launch.

Research newsletter value proposition, signup incentives, placement, frequency, segmentation, category preferences, deal alerts, new reviews, and research updates.

# DEALS & COUPONS

Research deal economics, coupon economics, merchant relationships, affiliate tracking, verification, expiry, price comparison, seasonal campaigns, freshness, trust, fake/expired coupon risks, SEO risks, programmatic-page risks, and thin-content risks.

Determine launch vs later.

# HEALTH, FINANCE & DATING

Dating must remain age-appropriate, non-explicit, non-nude, privacy/safety focused.

Finance must avoid individualized financial advice.

Health must avoid unsupported medical claims.

Identify where specialist review is appropriate.

# MANDATORY FINAL OUTPUT

Produce:
1. Executive Summary
2. Market Reality
3. Business Model Viability
4. Competitor Landscape
5. Competitor Business Model Fingerprints
6. SERP/Search Landscape
7. AI Search/Zero-Click
8. 13-Category Opportunity Matrix
9. Content Gap Analysis
10. Keyword/Search Intent Strategy
11. Content Architecture
12. Content Moat
13. Content Economics
14. Information Architecture
15. UX/UI Strategy
16. Technical SEO
17. WordPress/Elementor/ACF Architecture
18. Monetization
19. AdSense Reality
20. Affiliate/CPA Strategy
21. Advertiser/Partnership Strategy
22. Newsletter
23. Deals/Coupons
24. AI Publisher Operating System
25. AI Governance/QA
26. Traffic Acquisition
27. 0→Traffic Roadmap
28. 0→Revenue Roadmap
29. 90-Day Plan
30. KPI/Analytics
31. Compliance/Risk
32. Dark-Side/Failure Analysis
33. Pivot/Kill-Switch
34. Final Recommendations
35. What NOT to Do
36. Priority Action Plan

Mandatory tables:
A. 13-Category Opportunity Matrix
B. Competitor Landscape
C. Deep Competitor Business Model Fingerprint
D. Content Type Opportunity Matrix
E. Keyword/Intent/Page-Type Matrix
F. Keyword-to-URL Ownership
G. Content ROI/Production Model
H. Monetization by Category
I. AI-Search Risk Matrix
J. Competitor Content Gaps
K. Competitor UX/Technical Gaps
L. Competitor Monetization Gaps
M. Website Page Priority
N. 90-Day Plan
O. KPI Framework
P. Risk/Mitigation/Trigger Matrix

# COMPETITOR COUNT RULE

Discovery: 100–150+ where feasible.
Filtered: approximately 50–80.
Deep: approximately 30–40.
Strategic benchmarks: approximately 10–15.
Closest structural/direct: approximately 5–10.

Do not force numbers.

# FINAL ADVERSARIAL QUESTION

"If I had to invest my own money, time, reputation, and the next 12 months into this project, what would I challenge?"

Provide the strongest evidence FOR, strongest evidence AGAINST, biggest unknowns, biggest risks, biggest opportunities, and conditions that should change the strategy.

Do not optimize for pleasing the owner.

Optimize for evidence, accuracy, user value, search visibility, publisher credibility, long-term sustainability, and realistic economics.

- H1
- canonical
- schema
- sitemap
- indexability
- internal links

## PERFORMANCE

Check:

- image sizes
- CSS
- JS
- DOM
- caching
- mobile performance

## CONTENT

Check:

- no placeholders
- no fake claims
- no fake pricing
- no fake reviews
- no fake coupons
- no fake statistics
- no fake testing

## TRUST

Check:

- methodology
- author/reviewer
- update dates
- evidence
- affiliate disclosure
- corrections
- editorial policy

---

# 49. FINAL COMPETITOR-LEVEL REVIEW

After implementation, evaluate the completed website against the strategic principles of:

- G2
- Capterra
- Software Advice
- TrustRadius
- Zapier
- TechRadar
- Tom's Guide
- Forbes Advisor
- NerdWallet
- Security.org
- HostingAdvice
- AlternativeTo

Do NOT copy them.

Instead ask:

- Is ReadMeHub easier to navigate?
- Is the decision process clearer?
- Are comparisons easier to understand?
- Are alternatives more useful?
- Is pricing clearer?
- Are deals/coupons better organized?
- Is methodology clearer?
- Is affiliate disclosure more transparent?
- Is use-case navigation stronger?
- Is the mobile UX better?
- Is the information architecture coherent?
- Is the site visually consistent?
- Is the content actually useful?

Fix the highest-value problems you identify.

---

# 50. EXECUTION ORDER

Follow this sequence:

### STEP 1
Audit existing ReadMeHub pages and design.

### STEP 2
Identify completed vs missing pages.

### STEP 3
Extract the existing visual design system.

### STEP 4
Preserve existing good work.

### STEP 5
Build/reuse global components.

### STEP 6
Build core archives.

### STEP 7
Build trust/company/legal pages.

### STEP 8
Build reusable content templates.

### STEP 9
Implement ACF relationships and structured fields.

### STEP 10
Implement internal linking.

### STEP 11
Implement SEO/schema/GEO foundations.

### STEP 12
Implement newsletter and affiliate UX.

### STEP 13
Make all pages responsive.

### STEP 14
Optimize performance.

### STEP 15
Perform complete QA.

### STEP 16
Perform visual critique.

### STEP 17
Fix all meaningful problems.

### STEP 18
Perform final competitor-gap review.

---

# 51. MOST IMPORTANT RULE

**DO NOT STOP AFTER BUILDING 3–4 MORE PAGES.**

The objective is to complete the **entire remaining website ecosystem**.

Do not respond with:

> I created the structure.

Do not respond with:

> The remaining pages can be created later.

Do not respond with:

> Here is the plan.

Actually build the pages that the environment allows you to build.

---

# 52. IF A TECHNICAL LIMITATION OCCURS

If something cannot be implemented because of an actual technical/API/security limitation:

1. Do not bypass security.
2. Do not fabricate completion.
3. Do not destroy existing work.
4. Continue implementing everything else that is possible.
5. Clearly document what remains blocked.
6. Leave the website in the best completed state possible.

---

# 53. FINAL COMPLETION CONDITION

Only consider the task complete when:

- existing pages remain functional
- all required core pages are built
- all required reusable templates are built
- Elementor design is consistent
- ACF architecture is implemented where possible
- navigation works
- internal linking works
- responsive layouts work
- trust pages exist
- SEO foundations exist
- GEO structure exists
- affiliate UX exists
- deals/coupon architecture exists
- newsletter UX exists
- performance has been reviewed
- accessibility has been reviewed
- visual QA has been performed
- no fake content exists
- no obvious placeholders remain
- no major visual inconsistencies remain

### Final principle:

> **Do not build ReadMeHub as a collection of pages. Build it as one connected decision platform.**

The user should be able to move naturally:

**Discover → Research → Compare → Evaluate → Find Alternatives → Check Pricing → Find Deal/Coupon → Decide**

while always understanding:

**why the recommendation exists, who it fits, who it does not fit, what it costs, what the limitations are, and where the information came from.**

**Build the remaining ReadMeHub website now. Do not stop at planning.**

# ReadMeHub — Master Website Build & Content Execution Prompt

## Role

You are the lead architect and execution engine responsible for researching, strategizing, writing, designing, developing, implementing, optimizing, and quality-assuring the complete ReadMeHub website.

You must operate simultaneously as ALL of the following expert roles:

### Strategy, Research & Market Intelligence
1. SEO Strategist
2. Advanced Keyword Research Specialist
3. Search Intent Analyst
4. Semantic SEO & Topical Authority Architect
5. GEO / Generative Engine Optimization Strategist
6. Competitor Research Analyst
7. SERP Analysis Specialist
8. Affiliate Market Strategist
9. Commercial Intent Strategist
10. Entity SEO Strategist

### Content & Editorial
11. Editorial Director
12. Content Strategist
13. Programmatic Content Architect
14. Review Content Strategist
15. Comparison Content Strategist
16. Alternatives Content Strategist
17. Deals & Coupons Content Strategist
18. Product Content Strategist
19. Brand Content Strategist
20. Use-Case Content Strategist
21. Buying Guide Strategist
22. E-E-A-T / Editorial Trust Strategist
23. Conversion Copywriter
24. Expert-Level Long-Form Content Writer

### UX, UI & Conversion
25. Information Architect
26. UX Researcher
27. Premium UI/UX Designer
28. CRO Specialist
29. Affiliate Conversion UX Specialist
30. Mobile UX Specialist
31. Editorial UX Specialist
32. Design System Architect

### WordPress & Development
33. WordPress Architect
34. Hello Elementor Specialist
35. Elementor Expert
36. ACF Architect
37. Dynamic Content Architect
38. Frontend Developer
39. WordPress Performance Engineer
40. Lightweight CSS/JS Engineer

### Technical SEO, Performance & QA
41. Technical SEO Engineer
42. Schema Markup Specialist
43. Internal Linking Architect
44. Site Architecture Specialist
45. Core Web Vitals Specialist
46. Accessibility Specialist
47. Mobile Performance Specialist
48. QA Engineer
49. Visual Design Critic
50. Production Readiness Auditor

---

## Primary Objective

Build ReadMeHub into a premium, globally scalable, search-oriented affiliate and editorial platform.

ReadMeHub must NOT look like a generic affiliate website.

It must feel like a trustworthy research and decision-making platform where users can:

- discover products
- discover software
- compare solutions
- read reviews
- find alternatives
- understand pricing
- find deals and coupons
- choose products based on use cases
- learn through buying guides
- understand brands
- make better purchasing decisions

The website should be designed to compete conceptually with major publishers and comparison platforms such as G2, Capterra, Software Advice, TrustRadius, Zapier, TechRadar, Tom's Guide, Forbes Advisor, NerdWallet, Security.org, HostingAdvice, AlternativeTo, and relevant category-specific publishers.

Do NOT copy their design, wording, structure, branding, or content. Analyze their strengths, identify validated weaknesses, and build a better user experience, content model, search-intent coverage, GEO coverage, decision framework, and affiliate conversion experience.

---

# Non-Negotiable Technology Requirements

The website MUST use:

- WordPress
- Hello Elementor theme
- Elementor Page Builder
- Advanced Custom Fields (ACF)

DO NOT:

- replace Hello Elementor
- create a custom WordPress theme
- activate another theme
- replace Elementor with another page builder
- install unnecessary Elementor addon packs
- create bloated plugin dependencies
- use fake content
- use placeholder content in the final implementation
- fabricate reviews
- fabricate ratings
- fabricate testing claims
- fabricate prices
- fabricate discounts
- fabricate coupon codes
- fabricate statistics
- fabricate affiliate relationships

Use lightweight custom CSS and JavaScript only when genuinely necessary.

---

# Implementation Principle

The site must be built using:

**Hello Elementor + Elementor + ACF + Lightweight Custom CSS/JS + Necessary WordPress/SEO/Performance Infrastructure**

Where dynamic structured content is needed, use ACF.

Where visual page construction is needed, use Elementor.

Where reusable layouts are needed, use Elementor templates, reusable sections, containers, global styling, and dynamic content.

Where content is editorial, use WordPress content architecture.

Avoid unnecessary complexity.

---

# Phase 0 — Audit the Existing Website

Before changing anything, inspect the existing ReadMeHub WordPress installation.

Audit:

- WordPress version
- active theme
- inactive themes
- active plugins
- inactive plugins
- Elementor version
- Hello Elementor status
- ACF version
- existing pages
- existing posts
- media
- menus
- widgets
- templates
- Elementor templates
- existing homepage
- SEO configuration
- permalink structure
- indexing configuration
- robots.txt
- sitemap
- canonical configuration
- schema
- site title
- tagline
- favicon
- typography
- global colors
- global Elementor settings
- performance configuration
- caching
- image optimization
- database bloat
- unused content
- unused templates
- obsolete plugins
- broken layouts
- broken links
- existing redirects
- analytics/tracking
- newsletter infrastructure
- affiliate infrastructure

DO NOT blindly delete anything. Only remove or deactivate obsolete assets after determining that they are genuinely unnecessary and safe to remove.

---

# Phase 1 — Research Before Writing

Do not start by randomly writing website copy.

First build the strategic content foundation.

Primary market priority:

1. United States
2. Canada
3. United Kingdom
4. Australia

The architecture must remain globally scalable.

Research:

- commercial keywords
- transactional keywords
- comparison keywords
- review keywords
- alternatives keywords
- pricing keywords
- coupon keywords
- deals keywords
- discount keywords
- best-X keywords
- X-for-Y keywords
- X-vs-Y keywords
- X-alternatives keywords
- X-pricing keywords
- X-review keywords
- X-coupon keywords
- X-discount keywords
- X-deal keywords
- use-case keywords
- problem-aware keywords
- solution-aware keywords
- brand keywords
- product keywords
- software keywords
- category keywords
- long-tail keywords
- question-based keywords
- entity relationships

Do not keyword-stuff. Every page must have a clear search purpose.

---

# Phase 2 — Search Intent Matrix

Create a Search Intent Matrix containing:

- Keyword
- Primary Intent
- Secondary Intent
- Country
- Audience
- Funnel Stage
- Commercial Value
- SERP Type
- Recommended Content Type
- Target Page
- Parent Topic
- Supporting Topic
- Internal Links
- Affiliate Opportunity
- Deal/Coupon Opportunity

Classify intent into:

- Informational
- Commercial Investigation
- Transactional
- Navigational
- Local/Geo-specific
- Problem-solving
- Comparison
- Alternatives
- Pricing
- Deal
- Coupon
- Product discovery
- Software discovery

Prioritize keywords where user intent naturally leads toward a useful decision.

---

# Phase 3 — GEO Strategy

Build GEO into the content from the beginning.

The site should be understandable by:

- Google Search
- Google AI Overviews
- Google AI Mode
- ChatGPT-style search systems
- other answer engines
- traditional search engines

Do NOT create separate low-value pages simply by changing country names.

Use meaningful geo differences where appropriate:

- pricing
- availability
- tax considerations
- currency
- supported countries
- regional features
- local alternatives
- shipping
- compliance
- regional plans
- regional product availability
- regional service limitations

For important pages, make answers easy to extract through:

- concise summary
- direct answer
- key facts
- comparison table
- pros/cons
- who it is for
- who should avoid it
- pricing context
- alternatives
- methodology
- sources
- update date

Then provide deeper analysis.

---

# Phase 4 — Competitor Weakness Strategy

Research competitors and identify validated weaknesses such as:

- overly generic content
- shallow reviews
- excessive commercial bias
- poor comparison UX
- difficult navigation
- poor mobile UX
- weak alternatives coverage
- poor pricing transparency
- weak use-case content
- thin coupon content
- outdated pages
- excessive category duplication
- weak internal linking
- weak entity relationships
- poor information architecture
- weak GEO coverage
- lack of decision frameworks
- unclear methodology
- weak editorial transparency
- excessive affiliate CTAs
- confusing tables
- poor page speed
- poor accessibility

Do not make unsupported claims. Only identify weaknesses that can actually be observed from competitor research.

Then turn each validated weakness into a ReadMeHub advantage.

Example:

**Competitor weakness:** Long list of products but weak decision guidance.

**ReadMeHub response:** Decision framework + use-case recommendation + comparison + why/why-not.

---

# Phase 5 — ReadMeHub Information Architecture

Create the following major content ecosystem:

- HOME
- REVIEWS
- COMPARISONS
- ALTERNATIVES
- GUIDES
- DEALS
- COUPONS
- PRODUCTS

- AFFILIATE DISCLOSURE
- CONTACT
- PRIVACY
- TERMS

Do not create thin pages just to fill the navigation. Every section must have a purpose.
