# Phase 7 — Homepage Strategy

The homepage must NOT be a basic affiliate homepage.

It must be a premium editorial decision platform.

The homepage may be long. Do not shorten it merely to reduce page length.

The goal is:

**USEFULNESS > EMPTY WHITESPACE**

**DEPTH > GENERIC MARKETING**

**DECISION SUPPORT > RANDOM PRODUCT LISTS**

**TRUST > HYPE**

The homepage must contain real, meaningful content.

---

# Homepage Structure

## 1. Header

Create a premium clean header.

Navigation should provide access to:

- Reviews
- Comparisons
- Alternatives
- Deals
- Coupons
- Guides
- Products
- Brands
- Use Cases

Include:

- logo
- search
- navigation
- mobile navigation
- strategically placed CTA where justified

Do not overcrowd.

## 2. Hero

Position ReadMeHub as a decision/research platform.

Positioning direction:

> Research smarter. Compare better. Choose with confidence.

Supporting concept:

> Independent guides, reviews, comparisons, deals and practical recommendations that help you make better buying decisions.

Do not use this exact copy automatically if research indicates a stronger positioning.

Hero must include:

- strong headline
- supporting copy
- search interface
- primary discovery action
- secondary exploration action
- visual decision-oriented element

Search should conceptually support:

- products
- software
- brands
- reviews
- comparisons
- deals
- coupons
- guides

## 3. Decision Finder

Create an interactive decision discovery block.

Example:

**What are you trying to choose?**

- Software
- Hosting
- Security
- Productivity
- Business Tools
- AI Tools
- Consumer Products
- Other

Then:

**What matters most?**

- Price
- Features
- Ease of use
- Performance
- Security
- Best for beginners
- Best for business
- Best value

The interface should lead users to relevant content.

Do not build fake personalization. If true dynamic logic is not available, build clear category/intent navigation.

## 4. Featured Decisions

Create editorially selected decision cards.

Each card should contain:

- title
- short explanation
- category
- decision context
- link

Only use topics that are researched and supported.

## 5. Best Picks by Use Case

Create a visually strong grid.

Potential use cases:

- Best for beginners
- Best for small businesses
- Best value
- Best for teams
- Best for remote work
- Best for creators
- Best for agencies
- Best for advanced users

Avoid generic "best" claims unless supported by editorial methodology.

## 6. Popular Comparisons

Show high-value comparison pages.

Include:

- concise decision
- best for A
- best for B
- pricing context
- key difference
- CTA

## 7. Latest Reviews

Each review card should include:

- image
- category
- product
- short verdict
- updated date
- CTA

Do not display fake ratings. If rating methodology is not implemented, use qualitative verdicts.

## 8. Recently Updated

Show:

- recently updated reviews
- updated comparisons
- verified pricing
- updated deals
- refreshed guides

Each item should have a real date.

## 9. Explore Categories

Create visually rich category cards.

Potential categories may include:

- AI Tools
- Productivity
- Business Software
- Website Builders
- Web Hosting
- Cybersecurity
- Password Managers
- VPNs
- Marketing Tools
- Finance Tools
- Design Tools
- Communication
- Project Management
- E-commerce
- Consumer Technology

Do not automatically use all of these. Use research to determine the launch taxonomy.

## 10. Explore by Intent

This should be a major ReadMeHub differentiator.

Examples:

- I need a tool for...
- I want to compare...
- I'm looking for alternatives...
- I want the cheapest option...
- I need the best value...
- I need something for my team...
- I want a beginner-friendly option...
- I want a deal...
- I need a coupon...

## 11. Deals & Coupons

Deals & Coupons must be a first-class vertical.

Create:

- Featured Deals
- Verified Coupons
- Recently Checked Offers
- Category Deals
- Brand Deals
- Software Deals
- Hosting Deals
- Security Deals
- Seasonal Deals

Each offer must clearly distinguish:

- Verified
- Unverified
- Expired
- Official
- Affiliate

Never create fake coupons, discounts, prices, or urgency.

If there is no verified offer, say so.

## 12. Price & Value Discovery

Focus on:

- price transparency
- plan differences
- free vs paid
- entry-level pricing
- total value
- hidden limitations
- upgrade triggers

Prices must be sourced and date-verified when displayed.

## 13. Research & Evidence

Explain:

- how products are researched
- what sources are used
- how pricing is checked
- how features are compared
- when hands-on testing exists
- when testing does not exist
- how updates are verified
- how affiliate relationships are disclosed

Never imply hands-on testing unless it actually happened.

## 14. How We Evaluate

Potential evaluation dimensions:

- Features
- Value
- Ease of use
- Performance
- Security
- Support
- Integrations
- Availability
- User suitability
- Limitations

Weights must only be used if an actual scoring methodology exists.

## 15. Editorial Trust

Include:

- editorial principles
- affiliate disclosure
- methodology
- update policy
- correction policy
- source policy
- author/reviewer information
- distinction between editorial content and advertising

Do not make unsupported claims such as "100% independent" if affiliate relationships exist.

## 16. Guides / Learning Hub

Create:

- beginner guides
- buying guides
- how-to guides
- comparison guides
- terminology explainers
- setup guides
- troubleshooting
- decision frameworks

Use internal linking to commercial pages.

## 17. FAQ

Use FAQs only when genuinely useful.

Questions must come from:

- real search demand
- page content
- actual user concerns

Do not create FAQ sections merely for SEO.

## 18. Newsletter

Positioning direction:

> Get smarter buying decisions in your inbox.

Potential benefits:

- new reviews
- important comparisons
- verified deals
- useful tools
- buying guides
- major updates

Do not use deceptive newsletter copy.

## 19. Final CTA

End the homepage with a strong but non-aggressive discovery CTA.

Potential directions:

- Start your next decision.
- Find what fits your needs.

Use the strongest version supported by conversion research.

## 20. Footer

Include:

### Explore
- Reviews
- Comparisons
- Alternatives
- Deals
- Coupons
- Guides
- Products
- Brands
- Use Cases

### Company
- About
- How We Evaluate
- Editorial Policy
- Affiliate Disclosure
- Contact

### Legal
- Privacy
- Terms
- Cookie Policy where applicable

Include newsletter signup.

Social links only if actually active.

---

# Phase 8 — Full Content System

Do not stop at homepage copy.

Build the actual content ecosystem.

For every priority category, create:

1. Category Hub
2. Best-X Guide
3. Product Reviews
4. Comparisons
5. Alternatives
6. Pricing pages where justified
7. Deals
8. Coupons
9. Use Cases
10. Supporting informational guides

Create topic clusters rather than isolated articles.

Example:

## Category: Project Management Software

### Pillar
Best Project Management Software

### Supporting
- Best Project Management Software for Small Business
- Best Project Management Software for Teams
- Free Project Management Software
- Project Management Software Pricing
- Project Management Software Alternatives
- ClickUp vs Asana
- ClickUp vs Monday
- Asana vs Monday
- etc.

Only create pages where search demand, user value, and content differentiation justify them.

# Phase 9 — Review Content Template

Every serious review must follow a useful decision framework.

Structure:

- H1
- Executive Verdict
- Who This Is For
- Who Should Avoid It
- Key Takeaways
- Pricing
- Plans
- Core Features
- Feature-by-Feature Analysis
- Ease of Use
- Performance where measurable
- Security where relevant
- Integrations
- Support
- Pros
- Cons
- Alternatives
- Comparison
- Use Cases
- Editorial Methodology
- Sources
- Affiliate Disclosure
- Last Updated
- Final Verdict

The review must answer:

- What problem does this solve?
- Who should buy/use it?
- Who should not?
- What does it cost?
- What are the limitations?
- What are better alternatives?

---

# Phase 10 — Comparison Template

Comparison pages should begin with the answer.

Structure:

## Quick Verdict

### Best for A
...

### Best for B
...

### Choose A if
...

### Choose B if
...

Then:

- Pricing
- Features
- Ease of Use
- Performance
- Integrations
- Security
- Support
- Limitations
- Use Cases
- Pros & Cons
- Alternatives
- Final Verdict

Use comparison tables where they genuinely improve understanding.

---

# Phase 11 — Alternatives Template

Structure:

- Why users look for alternatives
- What the original product does well
- Where it may not fit
- Best alternatives
- Best alternative by use case
- Best budget alternative
- Best beginner alternative
- Best advanced alternative
- Comparison table
- How to choose
- Final recommendation

Do not simply list competitors.

---

# Phase 12 — Deal Page Template

Deal pages must clearly show:

- Offer
- Product
- Deal type
- Who qualifies
- Validity
- Region
- Price context
- Terms
- Verification date
- Official source
- Affiliate disclosure

If no active deal exists:

> Currently, we could not verify an active offer.

Do not manufacture scarcity.

---

# Phase 13 — Coupon Page Template

Coupon pages must include:

- Brand
- Code if valid
- Discount
- Conditions
- Expiration
- Last verified
- How to use
- Region
- Official destination
- Disclosure

Never publish invented coupon codes.

---

# Phase 14 — GEO Content Framework

For US:

- USD
- US availability
- US-specific plans
- relevant regulations where applicable

For Canada:

- CAD where available
- Canadian availability
- regional limitations

For UK:

- GBP where available
- UK availability
- VAT context where relevant

For Australia:

- AUD where available
- Australian availability
- regional limitations

Only create geo-specific pages where there is enough meaningful difference.

Avoid doorway-page behavior.

---

# Phase 15 — Internal Linking

Build a deliberate internal-linking graph.

Every commercial page should connect to:

- parent category
- related reviews
- comparisons
- alternatives
- guides
- use cases
- deals
- coupons
- relevant brands/products

Every informational page should have natural commercial pathways where relevant.

Use descriptive anchor text.

Do not spam internal links.

---

# Phase 16 — Schema

Implement structured data only when justified and visible on the page.

Potential schema:

- Organization
- WebSite
- BreadcrumbList
- Article
- Review
- Product
- AggregateRating only when legitimate
- Offer only when legitimate
- FAQPage only where technically appropriate and content genuinely qualifies
- ItemList where appropriate

Never fabricate:

- reviews
- ratings
- author information
- offers
- prices
- dates

Schema must match visible content.

---

# Phase 17 — Elementor Design System

Build a reusable design system in Elementor.

## Typography

Define:

- primary font
- heading scale
- body scale
- metadata scale
- button typography

## Spacing

Define:

- section spacing
- card spacing
- content width
- grid gaps
- mobile spacing

## Components

Build reusable components for:

- Header
- Hero
- Search
- Decision Finder
- Category Card
- Review Card
- Comparison Card
- Deal Card
- Coupon Card
- Product Card
- Brand Card
- Use Case Card
- Guide Card
- Author Card
- Trust Card
- Methodology Card
- CTA
- Newsletter
- Footer

Use Elementor Global Colors and Global Fonts.

Avoid excessive nested containers.

Use CSS classes for reusable styling.

---

# Phase 18 — Visual Design Direction

The visual language must be:

- premium
- editorial
- modern
- trustworthy
- clean
- information-rich
- highly scannable
- sophisticated
- conversion-aware

Do NOT make it look like:

- a coupon spam site
- an Amazon clone
- a generic SaaS template
- a blog with random cards
- a cheap affiliate site
- an Elementor demo

Use:

- strong typography
- clear hierarchy
- editorial layouts
- controlled card usage
- meaningful whitespace
- visual grouping
- subtle borders
- appropriate shadows
- strong CTA hierarchy
- comparison tables
- badges only when meaningful
- clear metadata
- visual trust signals

Do not use visual decoration merely for decoration.

---

# Phase 19 — Mobile First

Design every component for:

- Mobile
- Tablet
- Desktop
- Large desktop

Pay particular attention to:

- navigation
- search
- comparison tables
- deal cards
- coupon cards
- long-form content
- CTA placement
- font sizes
- tap targets
- sticky elements
- horizontal overflow
- image dimensions

Do not simply shrink desktop layouts. Create genuinely usable mobile layouts.

---

# Phase 20 — Performance

Performance is a core requirement.

Optimize for:

- Core Web Vitals
- LCP
- CLS
- INP
- image dimensions
- lazy loading
- responsive images
- font loading
- DOM size
- CSS
- JavaScript
- Elementor output
- plugin overhead
- database queries
- caching
- server response

Avoid:

- huge hero videos
- unnecessary animations
- heavy sliders
- excessive JavaScript
- unnecessary icon libraries
- unnecessary Elementor addons
- oversized images
- duplicate scripts

Use LiteSpeed Cache if already installed and appropriate.

---

# Phase 21 — Accessibility

Implement:

- semantic headings
- correct heading hierarchy
- alt text
- keyboard navigation
- visible focus
- sufficient contrast
- accessible forms
- accessible buttons
- descriptive links
- mobile tap targets

Do not use headings only for visual styling.

---

# Phase 22 — SEO Implementation

Every indexable page must have:

- unique title
- unique meta description
- single clear H1
- semantic headings
- canonical
- optimized URL
- internal links
- relevant schema
- useful content
- author/editor information where relevant
- updated date where relevant
- image optimization
- breadcrumb where appropriate

Avoid:

- keyword stuffing
- thin affiliate pages
- duplicate content
- doorway pages
- mass-generated useless pages
- fake reviews
- misleading claims

---

# Phase 23 — Content Quality Standard

Every article must satisfy:

1. Search intent
2. User usefulness
3. Original analysis
4. Clear structure
5. Strong first-hand perspective only when genuinely available
6. Transparent methodology
7. Accurate pricing
8. Accurate affiliate information
9. Meaningful internal links
10. Strong readability
11. GEO-friendly answer structure
12. No unsupported claims

Do not artificially increase word count.

LONG CONTENT IS ACCEPTABLE.

But every paragraph must earn its place.

---

# Phase 24 — Content Depth

Do not produce shallow affiliate articles when the topic requires comprehensive research.

For competitive commercial pages, content should be sufficiently comprehensive to answer:

- what
- why
- who
- how
- price
- alternatives
- comparison
- limitations
- use cases
- decision criteria

Use tables, bullets, examples and concise summaries to improve comprehension.

---

# Phase 25 — Authorship & Trust

Create:

- Author system
- Reviewer system where genuinely applicable
- Editorial policy
- Affiliate disclosure
- Correction policy
- Update methodology
- Research methodology

Do not invent expert credentials.

Do not invent authors.

Do not claim laboratory testing unless performed.

Do not claim "personally tested" unless actually tested.

---

# Phase 26 — Affiliate UX

Affiliate links must feel like a natural extension of useful content.

Do not bombard users with:

- BUY NOW
- GET DEAL
- CLICK HERE

Instead use contextual CTAs:

- Check current pricing
- Visit official site
- See available plans
- Compare options
- View current offer
- Explore alternatives

Affiliate disclosures should be clear.

---

# Phase 27 — Deals UX

Deals should be visually prominent without becoming spammy.

Each deal card should communicate:

- What
- How much
- Who
- When
- Where
- Verification status
- Terms
- Destination

Visual hierarchy:

**Brand → Offer → Short benefit → Verification badge → Expiration → CTA**

Never fake urgency.

---

# Phase 28 — Search Experience

Create a useful site-wide search experience.

Search should conceptually support:

- Products
- Brands
- Reviews
- Comparisons
- Deals
- Coupons
- Guides
- Categories
- Use Cases

Search results should be organized by content type where possible.

---

# Phase 29 — Entity Architecture

Build relationships:

**Brand → Products → Category → Reviews → Comparisons → Alternatives → Use Cases → Deals → Coupons → Guides**

This should create a coherent knowledge graph.

---

# Phase 30 — Category Hubs

Every major category should have a strong hub page.

Hub structure:

- Category introduction
- Best options
- Featured reviews
- Popular comparisons
- Alternatives
- Use cases
- Buying guides
- Deals
- Coupons
- Latest updates
- FAQ where useful

The category page should function as a navigation and decision center.

---

# Phase 31 — Content Prioritization

Prioritize pages based on:

- Search demand
- Commercial intent
- Affiliate value
- Competition
- SERP weakness
- Content differentiation
- Topical authority
- Internal linking value
- Update burden
- Geo opportunity

Use a scoring framework.

Do not publish hundreds of pages immediately. Start with the strongest opportunity cluster.

---

# Phase 32 — Launch Content

Create a launch roadmap.

### Tier 1
Highest-value money pages

### Tier 2
Supporting comparison/review pages

### Tier 3
Informational authority pages

### Tier 4
Deals/coupons

### Tier 5
Long-tail expansion

The exact pages must be determined from research.

---

# Phase 33 — Homepage Copywriting

Write complete final homepage copy.

DO NOT write:

- Lorem ipsum
- Coming soon
- Example
- Sample text
- Insert text here
- Add product
- Placeholder
- Your text here

All visible homepage text must be real.

Write:

- navigation labels
- hero headline
- hero paragraph
- search prompts
- section headings
- descriptions
- card labels
- CTA labels
- methodology copy
- trust copy
- newsletter copy
- footer copy
- legal navigation
- microcopy

---

# Phase 34 — Visual Content

Use images only where they improve understanding.

Do not fill empty space with meaningless stock images.

For product/software pages use, where legitimately available:

- product screenshots
- official logos where permitted
- relevant editorial visuals
- diagrams
- comparison graphics

Maintain consistent aspect ratios.

Optimize every image.

---

# Phase 35 — Production Implementation

After strategy and content are finalized:

**IMPLEMENT THE WEBSITE.**

Use:

- Hello Elementor
- Elementor
- ACF
- WordPress

Build:

- global styles
- header
- footer
- homepage
- templates
- archive layouts
- single content layouts
- cards
- category hubs
- review layout
- comparison layout
- deal layout
- coupon layout
- product layout
- brand layout
- use-case layout
- guide layout

Use reusable Elementor structures.

---

# Phase 36 — ACF Implementation

Create ACF field groups according to the final content architecture.

Use meaningful field names.

Avoid redundant fields.

Ensure:

- field labels are clear
- field types are correct
- conditional logic is used where useful
- fields are mapped correctly
- data can be reused in Elementor
- templates remain maintainable

---

# Phase 37 — Dynamic Content

Where appropriate, use Elementor dynamic fields to render ACF data.

Examples:

- Product name
- Brand
- Pricing
- Pros
- Cons
- Verification date
- Use cases
- Alternatives
- Official website
- Deals

Do not manually duplicate the same data across dozens of pages when it can be structured.

---

# Phase 38 — QA

Perform a full QA audit.

Check:

- Desktop
- Tablet
- Mobile

Check:

- header
- navigation
- search
- buttons
- cards
- links
- tables
- forms
- newsletter
- footer
- typography
- spacing
- alignment
- responsive behavior
- overflow
- broken layouts
- missing images
- alt text
- schema
- canonical
- metadata
- breadcrumbs
- internal links
- affiliate links
- deal links
- coupon links

---

# Phase 39 — Performance QA

Check:

- page weight
- image weight
- DOM complexity
- unnecessary CSS
- unnecessary JS
- Elementor bloat
- plugin bloat
- render-blocking resources
- caching
- mobile performance
- Core Web Vitals risks

Fix what can actually be fixed.

---

# Phase 40 — SEO QA

Check:

- indexability
- robots
- sitemap
- canonical
- title tags
- meta descriptions
- H1
- heading hierarchy
- breadcrumbs
- schema
- internal links
- orphan pages
- duplicate pages
- thin pages
- keyword cannibalization
- URL structure

---

# Phase 41 — GEO QA

Check whether important pages provide:

- concise answer
- direct verdict
- structured facts
- clear comparison
- clear pricing context
- entity names
- use cases
- limitations
- sources
- update dates

Content should be easy for answer engines to understand without sacrificing human readability.

---

# Phase 42 — Final Visual Critique

Act as a brutally honest senior UI/UX designer.

Review the rendered site.

Ask:

- Does it look premium?
- Does it look trustworthy?
- Does it look like a serious research platform?
- Does it look better organized than typical affiliate sites?
- Is there too much whitespace?
- Are cards repetitive?
- Are CTAs excessive?
- Is hierarchy obvious?
- Does the homepage feel empty?
- Does the homepage feel overloaded?
- Does mobile feel native?
- Are comparison tables readable?
- Are deals distinguishable?
- Are trust signals visible?
- Are users able to understand what ReadMeHub does within seconds?

Fix every meaningful issue.

---

# Phase 43 — Competitor Gap Audit

After implementation, compare ReadMeHub against the researched competitors.

Audit:

- Content depth
- Search intent coverage
- Comparison UX
- Review UX
- Deals UX
- Coupon UX
- Navigation
- Internal linking
- GEO
- Trust
- Methodology
- Mobile UX
- Performance
- Accessibility
- Visual design
- Conversion UX

Identify remaining gaps and fix the highest-value gaps.

---

# Phase 44 — Final Site Principles

The final website must communicate:

> ReadMeHub helps me make a decision.

NOT:

> This website wants me to click an affiliate link.

The user should feel:

- informed
- guided
- confident
- not pressured

---

# Anti-Spam Rules

Never:

- keyword stuff
- spin content
- rewrite competitors
- create pages only to target keyword variations
- publish fake reviews
- fabricate testing
- fabricate statistics
- fabricate coupons
- fabricate prices
- fabricate discounts
- create fake urgency
- create doorway pages
- create hundreds of near-identical geo pages
- hide affiliate relationships
- use misleading CTAs

---

# Content Originality Rule

Do not merely summarize competitors.

For every major page, provide original value through:

- decision frameworks
- comparisons
- structured analysis
- use-case segmentation
- pricing interpretation
- limitations
- alternatives
- contextual recommendations
- methodology
- fresh verification
- meaningful synthesis

---

# Execution Rule

Do not stop after creating a plan.

Do not stop after creating wireframes.

Do not stop after writing sample content.

Do not stop after building the homepage skeleton.

Execute the entire workflow as far as the available environment and permissions allow.

If a technical operation cannot be performed because of an actual system/API/security limitation:

1. Do NOT bypass security.
2. Do NOT fabricate success.
3. Clearly identify the exact limitation.
4. Continue completing everything else that can safely be completed.
5. Provide the exact remaining implementation requirement.

---

# Important: Do Not Ask Unnecessary Questions

Use reasonable expert judgment.

Do not repeatedly ask:

- What should I do next?
- What section should I build?
- Should I continue?
- Do you want me to proceed?

Proceed autonomously.

Only ask a question when the missing information genuinely blocks implementation.

---

# Final Deliverable

The final ReadMeHub system should include:

1. Strategic website architecture
2. Keyword/search-intent architecture
3. GEO strategy
4. Competitor-gap strategy
5. Complete content taxonomy
6. ACF data architecture
7. Elementor design system
8. Premium homepage
9. Reusable templates
10. Review architecture
11. Comparison architecture
12. Alternatives architecture
13. Deals architecture
14. Coupons architecture
15. Product architecture
16. Brand architecture
17. Use-case architecture
18. Guide architecture
19. Category hubs
20. Internal linking architecture
21. SEO architecture
22. Schema architecture
23. Affiliate UX
24. Newsletter UX
25. Mobile responsive design
26. Accessibility
27. Performance optimization
28. Technical QA
29. Visual QA
30. Content QA
31. GEO QA
32. Competitor gap audit

---

# Final Standard

Do not optimize ReadMeHub merely to rank.

Optimize it to become genuinely useful enough to deserve ranking.

Do not optimize merely for affiliate clicks.

Optimize for informed decisions.

Do not make the website longer simply to make it longer.

Make it deeper where depth improves decision quality.

Do not imitate competitors.

Exploit their validated weaknesses through better:

- information architecture
- decision support
- content depth
- transparency
- comparison UX
- use-case segmentation
- deals/coupons organization
- internal linking
- GEO
- mobile experience
- performance

The final product should feel like a combination of:

**Editorial Publication + Research Platform + Comparison Engine + Buying Guide + Deals Marketplace + Decision Assistant**

while remaining:

- fast
- trustworthy
- maintainable
- scalable
- SEO-friendly
- GEO-friendly

---

# Execution Sequence

## FIRST
Audit the existing ReadMeHub installation and available research/data.

## SECOND
Build the research, keyword, intent, competitor-gap and content architecture.

## THIRD
Finalize the information architecture and ACF data model.

## FOURTH
Write the actual content required for the initial launch pages.

## FIFTH
Design the complete Elementor system.

## SIXTH
Implement the website using Hello Elementor + Elementor + ACF.

## SEVENTH
Implement SEO, schema, internal linking, GEO, affiliate UX and performance.

## EIGHTH
Perform full desktop/tablet/mobile QA.

## NINTH
Perform competitor-gap and visual-quality audit.

## TENTH
Fix all meaningful issues discovered.

Do not declare the project complete unless the implementation and QA have actually been completed.

# ReadMeHub — Master Website Build & Content Execution Prompt

## Role

You are the lead architect and execution engine responsible for researching, strategizing, writing, designing, developing, implementing, optimizing, and quality-assuring the complete ReadMeHub website.

You must operate simultaneously as ALL of the following expert roles:

### Strategy, Research & Market Intelligence
1. SEO Strategist
2. Advanced Keyword Research Specialist
3. Search Intent Analyst
4. Semantic SEO & Topical Authority Architect
5. GEO / Generative Engine Optimization Strategist
6. Competitor Research Analyst
7. SERP Analysis Specialist
8. Affiliate Market Strategist
9. Commercial Intent Strategist
10. Entity SEO Strategist

### Content & Editorial
11. Editorial Director
12. Content Strategist
13. Programmatic Content Architect
14. Review Content Strategist
15. Comparison Content Strategist
16. Alternatives Content Strategist
17. Deals & Coupons Content Strategist
18. Product Content Strategist
19. Brand Content Strategist
20. Use-Case Content Strategist
21. Buying Guide Strategist
22. E-E-A-T / Editorial Trust Strategist
23. Conversion Copywriter
24. Expert-Level Long-Form Content Writer

### UX, UI & Conversion
25. Information Architect
26. UX Researcher
27. Premium UI/UX Designer
28. CRO Specialist
29. Affiliate Conversion UX Specialist
30. Mobile UX Specialist
31. Editorial UX Specialist
32. Design System Architect

### WordPress & Development
33. WordPress Architect
34. Hello Elementor Specialist
35. Elementor Expert
36. ACF Architect
37. Dynamic Content Architect
38. Frontend Developer
39. WordPress Performance Engineer
40. Lightweight CSS/JS Engineer

### Technical SEO, Performance & QA
41. Technical SEO Engineer
42. Schema Markup Specialist
43. Internal Linking Architect
44. Site Architecture Specialist
45. Core Web Vitals Specialist
46. Accessibility Specialist
47. Mobile Performance Specialist
48. QA Engineer
49. Visual Design Critic
50. Production Readiness Auditor

---

## Primary Objective

Build ReadMeHub into a premium, globally scalable, search-oriented affiliate and editorial platform.

ReadMeHub must NOT look like a generic affiliate website.

It must feel like a trustworthy research and decision-making platform where users can:

- discover products
- discover software
- compare solutions
- read reviews
- find alternatives
- understand pricing
- find deals and coupons
- choose products based on use cases
- learn through buying guides
- understand brands
- make better purchasing decisions

The website should be designed to compete conceptually with major publishers and comparison platforms such as G2, Capterra, Software Advice, TrustRadius, Zapier, TechRadar, Tom's Guide, Forbes Advisor, NerdWallet, Security.org, HostingAdvice, AlternativeTo, and relevant category-specific publishers.

Do NOT copy their design, wording, structure, branding, or content. Analyze their strengths, identify validated weaknesses, and build a better user experience, content model, search-intent coverage, GEO coverage, decision framework, and affiliate conversion experience.

---

# Non-Negotiable Technology Requirements

The website MUST use:

- WordPress
- Hello Elementor theme
- Elementor Page Builder
- Advanced Custom Fields (ACF)

DO NOT:

- replace Hello Elementor
- create a custom WordPress theme
- activate another theme
- replace Elementor with another page builder
- install unnecessary Elementor addon packs
- create bloated plugin dependencies
- use fake content
- use placeholder content in the final implementation
- fabricate reviews
- fabricate ratings
- fabricate testing claims
- fabricate prices
- fabricate discounts
- fabricate coupon codes
- fabricate statistics
- fabricate affiliate relationships

Use lightweight custom CSS and JavaScript only when genuinely necessary.

---

# Implementation Principle

The site must be built using:

**Hello Elementor + Elementor + ACF + Lightweight Custom CSS/JS + Necessary WordPress/SEO/Performance Infrastructure**

Where dynamic structured content is needed, use ACF.

Where visual page construction is needed, use Elementor.

Where reusable layouts are needed, use Elementor templates, reusable sections, containers, global styling, and dynamic content.

Where content is editorial, use WordPress content architecture.

Avoid unnecessary complexity.

---

# Phase 0 — Audit the Existing Website

Before changing anything, inspect the existing ReadMeHub WordPress installation.

Audit:

- WordPress version
- active theme
- inactive themes
- active plugins
- inactive plugins
- Elementor version
- Hello Elementor status
- ACF version
- existing pages
- existing posts
- media
- menus
- widgets
- templates
- Elementor templates
- existing homepage
- SEO configuration
- permalink structure
- indexing configuration
- robots.txt
- sitemap
- canonical configuration
- schema
- site title
- tagline
- favicon
- typography
- global colors
- global Elementor settings
- performance configuration
- caching
- image optimization
- database bloat
- unused content
- unused templates
- obsolete plugins
- broken layouts
- broken links
- existing redirects
- analytics/tracking
- newsletter infrastructure
- affiliate infrastructure

DO NOT blindly delete anything. Only remove or deactivate obsolete assets after determining that they are genuinely unnecessary and safe to remove.

---

# Phase 1 — Research Before Writing

Do not start by randomly writing website copy.

First build the strategic content foundation.

Primary market priority:

1. United States
2. Canada
3. United Kingdom
4. Australia

The architecture must remain globally scalable.

Research:

- commercial keywords
- transactional keywords
- comparison keywords
- review keywords
- alternatives keywords
- pricing keywords
- coupon keywords
- deals keywords
- discount keywords
- best-X keywords
- X-for-Y keywords
- X-vs-Y keywords
- X-alternatives keywords
- X-pricing keywords
- X-review keywords
- X-coupon keywords
- X-discount keywords
- X-deal keywords
- use-case keywords
- problem-aware keywords
- solution-aware keywords
- brand keywords
- product keywords
- software keywords
- category keywords
- long-tail keywords
- question-based keywords
- entity relationships

Do not keyword-stuff. Every page must have a clear search purpose.

---

# Phase 2 — Search Intent Matrix

Create a Search Intent Matrix containing:

- Keyword
- Primary Intent
- Secondary Intent
- Country
- Audience
- Funnel Stage
- Commercial Value
- SERP Type
- Recommended Content Type
- Target Page
- Parent Topic
- Supporting Topic
- Internal Links
- Affiliate Opportunity
- Deal/Coupon Opportunity

Classify intent into:

- Informational
- Commercial Investigation
- Transactional
- Navigational
- Local/Geo-specific
- Problem-solving
- Comparison
- Alternatives
- Pricing
- Deal
- Coupon
- Product discovery
- Software discovery

Prioritize keywords where user intent naturally leads toward a useful decision.

---

# Phase 3 — GEO Strategy

Build GEO into the content from the beginning.

The site should be understandable by:

- Google Search
- Google AI Overviews
- Google AI Mode
- ChatGPT-style search systems
- other answer engines
- traditional search engines

Do NOT create separate low-value pages simply by changing country names.

Use meaningful geo differences where appropriate:

- pricing
- availability
- tax considerations
- currency
- supported countries
- regional features
- local alternatives
- shipping
- compliance
- regional plans
- regional product availability
- regional service limitations

For important pages, make answers easy to extract through:

- concise summary
- direct answer
- key facts
- comparison table
- pros/cons
- who it is for
- who should avoid it
- pricing context
- alternatives
- methodology
- sources
- update date

Then provide deeper analysis.

---

# Phase 4 — Competitor Weakness Strategy

Research competitors and identify validated weaknesses such as:

- overly generic content
- shallow reviews
- excessive commercial bias
- poor comparison UX
- difficult navigation
- poor mobile UX
- weak alternatives coverage
- poor pricing transparency
- weak use-case content
- thin coupon content
- outdated pages
- excessive category duplication
- weak internal linking
- weak entity relationships
- poor information architecture
- weak GEO coverage
- lack of decision frameworks
- unclear methodology
- weak editorial transparency
- excessive affiliate CTAs
- confusing tables
- poor page speed
- poor accessibility

Do not make unsupported claims. Only identify weaknesses that can actually be observed from competitor research.

Then turn each validated weakness into a ReadMeHub advantage.

Example:

**Competitor weakness:** Long list of products but weak decision guidance.

**ReadMeHub response:** Decision framework + use-case recommendation + comparison + why/why-not.

---

# Phase 5 — ReadMeHub Information Architecture

Create the following major content ecosystem:

- HOME
- REVIEWS
- COMPARISONS
- ALTERNATIVES
- GUIDES
- DEALS
- COUPONS
- PRODUCTS
- BRANDS
- USE CASES

- EDITORIAL POLICY
- AFFILIATE DISCLOSURE
- CONTACT
- PRIVACY
- TERMS

Do not create thin pages just to fill the navigation. Every section must have a purpose.

# Phase 7 — Homepage Strategy

The homepage must NOT be a basic affiliate homepage.

It must be a premium editorial decision platform.

The homepage may be long. Do not shorten it merely to reduce page length.

The goal is:

**USEFULNESS > EMPTY WHITESPACE**

**DEPTH > GENERIC MARKETING**

**DECISION SUPPORT > RANDOM PRODUCT LISTS**

**TRUST > HYPE**

The homepage must contain real, meaningful content.

---

# Homepage Structure

## 1. Header

Create a premium clean header.

Navigation should provide access to:

- Reviews
- Comparisons
- Alternatives
- Deals
- Coupons
- Guides
- Products
- Brands
- Use Cases

Include:

- logo
- search
- navigation
- mobile navigation
- strategically placed CTA where justified

Do not overcrowd.

## 2. Hero

Position ReadMeHub as a decision/research platform.

Positioning direction:

> Research smarter. Compare better. Choose with confidence.

Supporting concept:

> Independent guides, reviews, comparisons, deals and practical recommendations that help you make better buying decisions.

Do not use this exact copy automatically if research indicates a stronger positioning.

Hero must include:

- strong headline
- supporting copy
- search interface
- primary discovery action
- secondary exploration action
- visual decision-oriented element

Search should conceptually support:

- products
- software
- brands
- reviews
- comparisons
- deals
- coupons
- guides

## 3. Decision Finder

Create an interactive decision discovery block.

Example:

**What are you trying to choose?**

- Software
- Hosting
- Security
- Productivity
- Business Tools
- AI Tools
- Consumer Products
- Other

Then:

**What matters most?**

- Price
- Features
- Ease of use
- Performance
- Security
- Best for beginners
- Best for business
- Best value

The interface should lead users to relevant content.

Do not build fake personalization. If true dynamic logic is not available, build clear category/intent navigation.

## 4. Featured Decisions

Create editorially selected decision cards.

Each card should contain:

- title
- short explanation
- category
- decision context
- link

Only use topics that are researched and supported.

## 5. Best Picks by Use Case

Create a visually strong grid.

Potential use cases:

- Best for beginners
- Best for small businesses
- Best value
- Best for teams
- Best for remote work
- Best for creators
- Best for agencies
- Best for advanced users

Avoid generic "best" claims unless supported by editorial methodology.

## 6. Popular Comparisons

Show high-value comparison pages.

Include:

- concise decision
- best for A
- best for B
- pricing context
- key difference
- CTA

## 7. Latest Reviews

Each review card should include:

- image
- category
- product
- short verdict
- updated date
- CTA

Do not display fake ratings. If rating methodology is not implemented, use qualitative verdicts.

## 8. Recently Updated

Show:

- recently updated reviews
- updated comparisons
- verified pricing
- updated deals
- refreshed guides

Each item should have a real date.

## 9. Explore Categories

Create visually rich category cards.

Potential categories may include:

- AI Tools
- Productivity
- Business Software
- Website Builders
- Web Hosting
- Cybersecurity
- Password Managers
- VPNs
- Marketing Tools
- Finance Tools
- Design Tools
- Communication
- Project Management
- E-commerce
- Consumer Technology

Do not automatically use all of these. Use research to determine the launch taxonomy.

## 10. Explore by Intent

This should be a major ReadMeHub differentiator.

Examples:

- I need a tool for...
- I want to compare...
- I'm looking for alternatives...
- I want the cheapest option...
- I need the best value...
- I need something for my team...
- I want a beginner-friendly option...
- I want a deal...
- I need a coupon...

## 11. Deals & Coupons

Deals & Coupons must be a first-class vertical.

Create:

- Featured Deals
- Verified Coupons
- Recently Checked Offers
- Category Deals
- Brand Deals
- Software Deals
- Hosting Deals
- Security Deals
- Seasonal Deals

Each offer must clearly distinguish:

- Verified
- Unverified
- Expired
- Official
- Affiliate

Never create fake coupons, discounts, prices, or urgency.

If there is no verified offer, say so.

## 12. Price & Value Discovery

Focus on:

- price transparency
- plan differences
- free vs paid
- entry-level pricing
- total value
- hidden limitations
- upgrade triggers

Prices must be sourced and date-verified when displayed.

## 13. Research & Evidence

Explain:

- how products are researched
- what sources are used
- how pricing is checked
- how features are compared
- when hands-on testing exists
- when testing does not exist
- how updates are verified
- how affiliate relationships are disclosed

Never imply hands-on testing unless it actually happened.

## 14. How We Evaluate

Potential evaluation dimensions:

- Features
- Value
- Ease of use
- Performance
- Security
- Support
- Integrations
- Availability
- User suitability
- Limitations

Weights must only be used if an actual scoring methodology exists.

## 15. Editorial Trust

Include:

- editorial principles
- affiliate disclosure
- methodology
- update policy
- correction policy
- source policy
- author/reviewer information
- distinction between editorial content and advertising

Do not make unsupported claims such as "100% independent" if affiliate relationships exist.

## 16. Guides / Learning Hub

Create:

- beginner guides
- buying guides
- how-to guides
- comparison guides
- terminology explainers
- setup guides
- troubleshooting
- decision frameworks

Use internal linking to commercial pages.

## 17. FAQ

Use FAQs only when genuinely useful.

Questions must come from:

- real search demand
- page content
- actual user concerns

Do not create FAQ sections merely for SEO.

## 18. Newsletter

Positioning direction:

> Get smarter buying decisions in your inbox.

Potential benefits:

- new reviews
- important comparisons
- verified deals
- useful tools
- buying guides
- major updates

Do not use deceptive newsletter copy.

## 19. Final CTA

End the homepage with a strong but non-aggressive discovery CTA.

Potential directions:

- Start your next decision.
- Find what fits your needs.

Use the strongest version supported by conversion research.

## 20. Footer

Include:

### Explore
- Reviews
- Comparisons
- Alternatives
- Deals
- Coupons
- Guides
- Products
- Brands
- Use Cases

### Company
- About
- How We Evaluate
- Editorial Policy
- Affiliate Disclosure
- Contact

### Legal
- Privacy
- Terms
- Cookie Policy where applicable

Include newsletter signup.

Social links only if actually active.

---

# Phase 8 — Full Content System

Do not stop at homepage copy.

Build the actual content ecosystem.

For every priority category, create:

1. Category Hub
2. Best-X Guide
3. Product Reviews
4. Comparisons
5. Alternatives
6. Pricing pages where justified
7. Deals
8. Coupons
9. Use Cases
10. Supporting informational guides

Create topic clusters rather than isolated articles.

Example:

## Category: Project Management Software

### Pillar
Best Project Management Software

### Supporting
- Best Project Management Software for Small Business
- Best Project Management Software for Teams
- Free Project Management Software
- Project Management Software Pricing
- Project Management Software Alternatives
- ClickUp vs Asana
- ClickUp vs Monday
- Asana vs Monday
- etc.

Only create pages where search demand, user value, and content differentiation justify them.

---

# Phase 9 — Review Content Template

Every serious review must follow a useful decision framework.

Structure:

- H1
- Executive Verdict
- Who This Is For
- Who Should Avoid It
- Key Takeaways
- Pricing
- Plans
- Core Features
- Feature-by-Feature Analysis
- Ease of Use
- Performance where measurable
- Security where relevant
- Integrations
- Support
- Pros
- Cons
- Alternatives
- Comparison
- Use Cases
- Editorial Methodology
- Sources
- Affiliate Disclosure
- Last Updated
- Final Verdict

The review must answer:

- What problem does this solve?
- Who should buy/use it?
- Who should not?
- What does it cost?
- What are the limitations?
- What are better alternatives?

---

# Phase 10 — Comparison Template

Comparison pages should begin with the answer.

Structure:

## Quick Verdict

### Best for A
...

### Best for B
...

### Choose A if
...

### Choose B if
...

Then:

- Pricing
- Features
- Ease of Use
- Performance
- Integrations
- Security
- Support
- Limitations
- Use Cases
- Pros & Cons
- Alternatives
- Final Verdict

Use comparison tables where they genuinely improve understanding.

---

# Phase 11 — Alternatives Template

Structure:

- Why users look for alternatives
- What the original product does well
- Where it may not fit
- Best alternatives
- Best alternative by use case
- Best budget alternative
- Best beginner alternative
- Best advanced alternative
- Comparison table
- How to choose
- Final recommendation

Do not simply list competitors.

---

# Phase 12 — Deal Page Template

Deal pages must clearly show:

- Offer
- Product
- Deal type
- Who qualifies
- Validity
- Region
- Price context
- Terms
- Verification date
- Official source
- Affiliate disclosure

If no active deal exists:

> Currently, we could not verify an active offer.

Do not manufacture scarcity.

---

# Phase 13 — Coupon Page Template

Coupon pages must include:

- Brand
- Code if valid
- Discount
- Conditions
- Expiration
- Last verified
- How to use
- Region
- Official destination
- Disclosure

Never publish invented coupon codes.

---

# Phase 14 — GEO Content Framework

For US:

- USD
- US availability
- US-specific plans
- relevant regulations where applicable

For Canada:

- CAD where available
- Canadian availability
- regional limitations

For UK:

- GBP where available
- UK availability
- VAT context where relevant

For Australia:

- AUD where available
- Australian availability
- regional limitations

Only create geo-specific pages where there is enough meaningful difference.

Avoid doorway-page behavior.

---

# Phase 15 — Internal Linking

Build a deliberate internal-linking graph.

Every commercial page should connect to:

- parent category
- related reviews
- comparisons
- alternatives
- guides
- use cases
- deals
- coupons
- relevant brands/products

Every informational page should have natural commercial pathways where relevant.

Use descriptive anchor text.

Do not spam internal links.

---

# Phase 16 — Schema

Implement structured data only when justified and visible on the page.

Potential schema:

- Organization
- WebSite
- BreadcrumbList
- Article
- Review
- Product
- AggregateRating only when legitimate
- Offer only when legitimate
- FAQPage only where technically appropriate and content genuinely qualifies
- ItemList where appropriate

Never fabricate:

- reviews
- ratings
- author information
- offers
- prices
- dates

Schema must match visible content.

---

# Phase 17 — Elementor Design System

Build a reusable design system in Elementor.

## Typography

Define:

- primary font
- heading scale
- body scale
- metadata scale
- button typography

## Spacing

Define:

- section spacing
- card spacing
- content width
- grid gaps
- mobile spacing

## Components

Build reusable components for:

- Header
- Hero
- Search
- Decision Finder
- Category Card
- Review Card
- Comparison Card
- Deal Card
- Coupon Card
- Product Card
- Brand Card
- Use Case Card
- Guide Card
- Author Card
- Trust Card
- Methodology Card
- CTA
- Newsletter
- Footer

Use Elementor Global Colors and Global Fonts.

Avoid excessive nested containers.

Use CSS classes for reusable styling.

---

# Phase 18 — Visual Design Direction

The visual language must be:

- premium
- editorial
- modern
- trustworthy
- clean
- information-rich
- highly scannable
- sophisticated
- conversion-aware

Do NOT make it look like:

- a coupon spam site
- an Amazon clone
- a generic SaaS template
- a blog with random cards
- a cheap affiliate site
- an Elementor demo

Use:

- strong typography
- clear hierarchy
- editorial layouts
- controlled card usage
- meaningful whitespace
- visual grouping
- subtle borders
- appropriate shadows
- strong CTA hierarchy
- comparison tables
- badges only when meaningful
- clear metadata
- visual trust signals

Do not use visual decoration merely for decoration.

---

# Phase 19 — Mobile First

Design every component for:

- Mobile
- Tablet
- Desktop
- Large desktop

Pay particular attention to:

- navigation
- search
- comparison tables
- deal cards
- coupon cards
- long-form content
- CTA placement
- font sizes
- tap targets
- sticky elements
- horizontal overflow
- image dimensions

Do not simply shrink desktop layouts. Create genuinely usable mobile layouts.

---

# Phase 20 — Performance

Performance is a core requirement.

Optimize for:

- Core Web Vitals
- LCP
- CLS
- INP
- image dimensions
- lazy loading
- responsive images
- font loading
- DOM size
- CSS
- JavaScript
- Elementor output
- plugin overhead
- database queries
- caching
- server response

Avoid:

- huge hero videos
- unnecessary animations
- heavy sliders
- excessive JavaScript
- unnecessary icon libraries
- unnecessary Elementor addons
- oversized images
- duplicate scripts

Use LiteSpeed Cache if already installed and appropriate.

---

# Phase 21 — Accessibility

Implement:

- semantic headings
- correct heading hierarchy
- alt text
- keyboard navigation
- visible focus
- sufficient contrast
- accessible forms
- accessible buttons
- descriptive links
- mobile tap targets

Do not use headings only for visual styling.

---

# Phase 22 — SEO Implementation

Every indexable page must have:

- unique title
- unique meta description
- single clear H1
- semantic headings
- canonical
- optimized URL
- internal links
- relevant schema
- useful content
- author/editor information where relevant
- updated date where relevant
- image optimization
- breadcrumb where appropriate

Avoid:

- keyword stuffing
- thin affiliate pages
- duplicate content
- doorway pages
- mass-generated useless pages
- fake reviews
- misleading claims

---

# Phase 23 — Content Quality Standard

Every article must satisfy:

1. Search intent
2. User usefulness
3. Original analysis
4. Clear structure
5. Strong first-hand perspective only when genuinely available
6. Transparent methodology
7. Accurate pricing
8. Accurate affiliate information
9. Meaningful internal links
10. Strong readability
11. GEO-friendly answer structure
12. No unsupported claims

Do not artificially increase word count.

LONG CONTENT IS ACCEPTABLE.

But every paragraph must earn its place.

---

# Phase 24 — Content Depth

Do not produce shallow affiliate articles when the topic requires comprehensive research.

For competitive commercial pages, content should be sufficiently comprehensive to answer:

- what
- why
- who
- how
- price
- alternatives
- comparison
- limitations
- use cases
- decision criteria

Use tables, bullets, examples and concise summaries to improve comprehension.

---

# Phase 25 — Authorship & Trust

Create:

- Author system
- Reviewer system where genuinely applicable
- Editorial policy
- Affiliate disclosure
- Correction policy
- Update methodology
- Research methodology

Do not invent expert credentials.

Do not invent authors.

Do not claim laboratory testing unless performed.

Do not claim "personally tested" unless actually tested.

---

# Phase 26 — Affiliate UX

Affiliate links must feel like a natural extension of useful content.

Do not bombard users with:

- BUY NOW
- GET DEAL
- CLICK HERE

Instead use contextual CTAs:

- Check current pricing
- Visit official site
- See available plans
- Compare options
- View current offer
- Explore alternatives

Affiliate disclosures should be clear.

---

# Phase 27 — Deals UX

Deals should be visually prominent without becoming spammy.

Each deal card should communicate:

- What
- How much
- Who
- When
- Where
- Verification status
- Terms
- Destination

Visual hierarchy:

**Brand → Offer → Short benefit → Verification badge → Expiration → CTA**

Never fake urgency.

---

# Phase 28 — Search Experience

Create a useful site-wide search experience.

Search should conceptually support:

- Products
- Brands
- Reviews
- Comparisons
- Deals
- Coupons
- Guides
- Categories
- Use Cases

Search results should be organized by content type where possible.

---

# Phase 29 — Entity Architecture

Build relationships:

**Brand → Products → Category → Reviews → Comparisons → Alternatives → Use Cases → Deals → Coupons → Guides**

This should create a coherent knowledge graph.

---

# Phase 30 — Category Hubs

Every major category should have a strong hub page.

Hub structure:

- Category introduction
- Best options
- Featured reviews
- Popular comparisons
- Alternatives
- Use cases
- Buying guides
- Deals
- Coupons
- Latest updates
- FAQ where useful

The category page should function as a navigation and decision center.

---

# Phase 31 — Content Prioritization

Prioritize pages based on:

- Search demand
- Commercial intent
- Affiliate value
- Competition
- SERP weakness
- Content differentiation
- Topical authority
- Internal linking value
- Update burden
- Geo opportunity

Use a scoring framework.

Do not publish hundreds of pages immediately. Start with the strongest opportunity cluster.

---

# Phase 32 — Launch Content

Create a launch roadmap.

### Tier 1
Highest-value money pages

### Tier 2
Supporting comparison/review pages

### Tier 3
Informational authority pages

### Tier 4
Deals/coupons

### Tier 5
Long-tail expansion

The exact pages must be determined from research.

---

# Phase 33 — Homepage Copywriting

Write complete final homepage copy.

DO NOT write:

- Lorem ipsum
- Coming soon
- Example
- Sample text
- Insert text here
- Add product
- Placeholder
- Your text here

All visible homepage text must be real.

Write:

- navigation labels
- hero headline
- hero paragraph
- search prompts
- section headings
- descriptions
- card labels
- CTA labels
- methodology copy
- trust copy
- newsletter copy
- footer copy
- legal navigation
- microcopy

---

# Phase 34 — Visual Content

Use images only where they improve understanding.

Do not fill empty space with meaningless stock images.

For product/software pages use, where legitimately available:

- product screenshots
- official logos where permitted
- relevant editorial visuals
- diagrams
- comparison graphics

Maintain consistent aspect ratios.

Optimize every image.

---

# Phase 35 — Production Implementation

After strategy and content are finalized:

**IMPLEMENT THE WEBSITE.**

Use:

- Hello Elementor
- Elementor
- ACF
- WordPress

Build:

- global styles
- header
- footer
- homepage
- templates
- archive layouts
- single content layouts
- cards
- category hubs
- review layout
- comparison layout
- deal layout
- coupon layout
- product layout
- brand layout
- use-case layout
- guide layout

Use reusable Elementor structures.

---

# Phase 36 — ACF Implementation

Create ACF field groups according to the final content architecture.

Use meaningful field names.

Avoid redundant fields.

Ensure:

- field labels are clear
- field types are correct
- conditional logic is used where useful
- fields are mapped correctly
- data can be reused in Elementor
- templates remain maintainable

---

# Phase 37 — Dynamic Content

Where appropriate, use Elementor dynamic fields to render ACF data.

Examples:

- Product name
- Brand
- Pricing
- Pros
- Cons
- Verification date
- Use cases
- Alternatives
- Official website
- Deals

Do not manually duplicate the same data across dozens of pages when it can be structured.

---

# Phase 38 — QA

Perform a full QA audit.

Check:

- Desktop
- Tablet
- Mobile

Check:

- header
- navigation
- search
- buttons
- cards
- links
- tables
- forms
- newsletter
- footer
- typography
- spacing
- alignment
- responsive behavior
- overflow
- broken layouts
- missing images
- alt text
- schema
- canonical
- metadata
- breadcrumbs
- internal links
- affiliate links
- deal links
- coupon links

---

# Phase 39 — Performance QA

Check:

- page weight
- image weight
- DOM complexity
- unnecessary CSS
- unnecessary JS
- Elementor bloat
- plugin bloat
- render-blocking resources
- caching
- mobile performance
- Core Web Vitals risks

Fix what can actually be fixed.

---

# Phase 40 — SEO QA

Check:

- indexability
- robots
- sitemap
- canonical
- title tags
- meta descriptions
- H1
- heading hierarchy
- breadcrumbs
- schema
- internal links
- orphan pages
- duplicate pages
- thin pages
- keyword cannibalization
- URL structure

---

# Phase 41 — GEO QA

Check whether important pages provide:

- concise answer
- direct verdict
- structured facts
- clear comparison
- clear pricing context
- entity names
- use cases
- limitations
- sources
- update dates

Content should be easy for answer engines to understand without sacrificing human readability.

---

# Phase 42 — Final Visual Critique

Act as a brutally honest senior UI/UX designer.

Review the rendered site.

Ask:

- Does it look premium?
- Does it look trustworthy?
- Does it look like a serious research platform?
- Does it look better organized than typical affiliate sites?
- Is there too much whitespace?
- Are cards repetitive?
- Are CTAs excessive?
- Is hierarchy obvious?
- Does the homepage feel empty?
- Does the homepage feel overloaded?
- Does mobile feel native?
- Are comparison tables readable?
- Are deals distinguishable?
- Are trust signals visible?
- Are users able to understand what ReadMeHub does within seconds?

Fix every meaningful issue.

---

# Phase 43 — Competitor Gap Audit

After implementation, compare ReadMeHub against the researched competitors.

Audit:

- Content depth
- Search intent coverage
- Comparison UX
- Review UX
- Deals UX
- Coupon UX
- Navigation
- Internal linking
- GEO
- Trust
- Methodology
- Mobile UX
- Performance
- Accessibility
- Visual design
- Conversion UX

Identify remaining gaps and fix the highest-value gaps.

---

# Phase 44 — Final Site Principles

The final website must communicate:

> ReadMeHub helps me make a decision.

NOT:

> This website wants me to click an affiliate link.

The user should feel:

- informed
- guided
- confident
- not pressured

---

# Anti-Spam Rules

Never:

- keyword stuff
- spin content
- rewrite competitors
- create pages only to target keyword variations
- publish fake reviews
- fabricate testing
- fabricate statistics
- fabricate coupons
- fabricate prices
- fabricate discounts
- create fake urgency
- create doorway pages
- create hundreds of near-identical geo pages
- hide affiliate relationships
- use misleading CTAs

---

# Content Originality Rule

Do not merely summarize competitors.

For every major page, provide original value through:

- decision frameworks
- comparisons
- structured analysis
- use-case segmentation
- pricing interpretation
- limitations
- alternatives
- contextual recommendations
- methodology
- fresh verification
- meaningful synthesis

---

# Execution Rule

Do not stop after creating a plan.

Do not stop after creating wireframes.

Do not stop after writing sample content.

Do not stop after building the homepage skeleton.

Execute the entire workflow as far as the available environment and permissions allow.

If a technical operation cannot be performed because of an actual system/API/security limitation:

1. Do NOT bypass security.
2. Do NOT fabricate success.
3. Clearly identify the exact limitation.
4. Continue completing everything else that can safely be completed.
5. Provide the exact remaining implementation requirement.

---

# Important: Do Not Ask Unnecessary Questions

Use reasonable expert judgment.

Do not repeatedly ask:

- What should I do next?
- What section should I build?
- Should I continue?
- Do you want me to proceed?

Proceed autonomously.

Only ask a question when the missing information genuinely blocks implementation.

---

# Final Deliverable

The final ReadMeHub system should include:

1. Strategic website architecture
2. Keyword/search-intent architecture
3. GEO strategy
4. Competitor-gap strategy
5. Complete content taxonomy
6. ACF data architecture
7. Elementor design system
8. Premium homepage
9. Reusable templates
10. Review architecture
11. Comparison architecture
12. Alternatives architecture
13. Deals architecture
14. Coupons architecture
15. Product architecture
16. Brand architecture
17. Use-case architecture
18. Guide architecture
19. Category hubs
20. Internal linking architecture
21. SEO architecture
22. Schema architecture
23. Affiliate UX
24. Newsletter UX
25. Mobile responsive design
26. Accessibility
27. Performance optimization
28. Technical QA
29. Visual QA
30. Content QA
31. GEO QA
32. Competitor gap audit

---

# Final Standard

Do not optimize ReadMeHub merely to rank.

Optimize it to become genuinely useful enough to deserve ranking.

Do not optimize merely for affiliate clicks.

Optimize for informed decisions.

Do not make the website longer simply to make it longer.

Make it deeper where depth improves decision quality.

Do not imitate competitors.

Exploit their validated weaknesses through better:

- information architecture
- decision support
- content depth
- transparency
- comparison UX
- use-case segmentation
- deals/coupons organization
- internal linking
- GEO
- mobile experience
- performance

The final product should feel like a combination of:

**Editorial Publication + Research Platform + Comparison Engine + Buying Guide + Deals Marketplace + Decision Assistant**

while remaining:

- fast
- trustworthy
- maintainable
- scalable
- SEO-friendly
- GEO-friendly

---

# Execution Sequence

## FIRST
Audit the existing ReadMeHub installation and available research/data.

## SECOND
Build the research, keyword, intent, competitor-gap and content architecture.

## THIRD
Finalize the information architecture and ACF data model.

## FOURTH
Write the actual content required for the initial launch pages.

## FIFTH
Design the complete Elementor system.

## SIXTH
Implement the website using Hello Elementor + Elementor + ACF.

## SEVENTH
Implement SEO, schema, internal linking, GEO, affiliate UX and performance.

## EIGHTH
Perform full desktop/tablet/mobile QA.

## NINTH
Perform competitor-gap and visual-quality audit.

## TENTH
Fix all meaningful issues discovered.

Do not declare the project complete unless the implementation and QA have actually been completed.

### Source file 35: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL (1).md`

---
name: readmehub-project-pack
description: "ReadMeHub project guidance (Layer B). Use whenever the task concerns ReadMeHub or www.readmehub.com, its AI tools directory, tool reviews, comparisons, deals and coupons, blog templates, Google Stitch screenshots for this site, or its editorial and affiliate rules. Supplies verified stack facts, design constraints, content-trust rules, template scope and acceptance criteria, and tells you which core skills to chain. Load only for ReadMeHub work."
---

# ReadMeHub Project Pack

Project facts and constraints for ReadMeHub. Generic method lives in the core skills; this pack only adds what is specific to this site.

## Verified stack (read-only checks, 2026-10-22)
WordPress 7.1.3, PHP 8.2.34, Hello Elementor 3.5.1, **Elementor free 4.3.5**, Rank Math 1.0.280, Xpro Elementor Addons 1.7.11, Xpro Theme Builder 1.2.16, Royal MCP 1.5.10 (connector). **ACF is not installed. Elementor Pro is not installed** (premium widgets in the registry are unverified stubs). The WPForms widget is registered but the WPForms plugin was not in the plugin list. Install age was 0 days on first probe, 1 day on 2026-10-23. Re-verify before relying on any of this; older project notes (Elementor 4.2.4, ACF 6.8.9) are stale.

## Environment rule
Project docs call www.readmehub.com staging. Weak signals observed read-only on 2026-10-23: robots `noindex, nofollow`, install age 1 day, one default post, no pages, only the Default Kit template, default site title "My Blog". None of these proves staging. Treat it as `UNVERIFIED` (production-grade caution) until the owner confirms a staging URL. Build on draft clones; never publish. Chain `wp-change-safety` first.

## Chain by task
- New or changed section from a screenshot: `design-system-to-elementor` (once) -> `stitch-to-elementor-plan` -> `wp-change-safety` -> `elementor-native-builder` -> `elementor-qa`.
- Template needing data: add `wp-dynamic-content-model` before the plan.
- Launch review: `elementor-qa` + `wp-performance-seo-qa`.

## Design constraints
Approved Stitch homepage and existing site styles are canonical. Do not create a competing identity or alter global styles to fix a local issue. Reference tokens (unconfirmed until checked against Stitch and the Elementor kit) are in `references/design-reference-tokens.md`. Layouts may differ by content type, but share one visual language. Reuse shared components; do not clone a component with different styling without a functional reason.

## Content trust (non-negotiable)
Never invent or imply: ratings, reviews, testimonials, prices, discounts, coupon codes, expiry dates, author credentials, company history, user counts, testing claims, or verification. Prices, features and offers need a source and a last-verified date. Optional data disappears gracefully. Unverified offers must not look verified; expired offers must not look active. Affiliate relationships must be disclosed accurately. Details in `references/content-trust-and-templates.md`.

## Template scope
17 planned templates (directory, tool review, blog archive/single, category, comparisons archive/single, deals archive/single, search, About, Methodology, Affiliate Disclosure, Contact/Report error, Privacy, Terms, 404). This is a checklist, not proof any exist. Read `references/content-trust-and-templates.md` when planning any of them.

## Acceptance criteria (every ReadMeHub task)
1. Draft only, correct page, Change Gate recorded.
2. Native Elementor/installed Xpro widgets; no page-sized HTML widget.
3. Tokens reused; no new global tokens without approval.
4. Text, images, buttons editable.
5. No unsupported facts; placeholders labeled.
6. Responsive status reported per viewport with evidence or `NOT-TESTED`/`BLOCKED`.
7. Build Report and QA Report delivered.

## Missing source
`06_CLAUDE_PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md` is not in the project files. Do not reconstruct it; the pasted project instructions are the working equivalent.

### Source file 36: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL (3)(1).md`

---
name: design-system-to-elementor
description: "Extract a design system (colors, fonts, type scale, spacing, radii, shadows, buttons, cards, breakpoints) from Google Stitch screenshots, a Figma or style export, design docs, or an existing site, and map it to the site's actual Elementor global settings. Use when the user says design tokens, design system, brand colors, global styles, typography scale, match the existing style, extract the palette, or before implementing any screenshot where tokens must be reused rather than invented."
---

# Design System to Elementor

Output: a **Token Inventory** and a **Token-to-Elementor Mapping**, with every value labeled by evidence. Never invent a palette.

## Sources, in priority order
1. Values the owner stated or approved design-system docs.
2. Actual Elementor site settings (active kit global colors/typography, theme customizer values), read through a connected tool or exported JSON.
3. Computed styles from the live page, if a browser is available.
4. Values sampled from screenshots (always `ESTIMATE`; say how sampled).

If sources conflict, record both and ask which is canonical. Do not merge silently.

## Workflow
1. List what you can actually read: connected WP/Elementor tools, browser, files. Mark each `AVAILABLE` or `NOT AVAILABLE`.
2. Read the kit (not `wp_get_global_styles`, which returns WordPress core defaults on classic themes): find the active kit post, read its settings (global colors, typography, container width, breakpoints, button defaults). Check whether this Elementor version exposes extra token systems (variables, global classes); do not assume.
3. Extract tokens from the reference into this table:
```
| Token | Value | Source | Status (VERIFIED/ESTIMATE/CONFLICT) | Existing Elementor global? | Action |
```
Cover: colors (brand, text, muted, border, surfaces, status), fonts and weights, heading/body scale with line height, container widths, section and card spacing, radii, shadows, button variants and states, input styles, icons, breakpoints.
4. Map each token: `REUSE global X`, `MATCHES a global within tolerance`, `NEW (needs approval)`, or `LOCAL ONLY (scoped override)`.
5. Inventory reusable components (header, footer, cards, badges, CTA blocks): where they exist (template ID or page) and whether they are real templates or copies.
6. List unresolved ambiguities as questions with a recommended default.

## Rules
- Reuse before creating. Adding or editing a global token is R3 under `wp-change-safety`; propose it, do not do it.
- A tolerance match (screenshot `#112234` vs global `#112233`) maps to the global and is noted.
- Fonts: confirm they are loaded on the site (kit or theme) before assuming they render.
- Hover, focus, and mobile states are usually not visible in a screenshot. Mark them `NOT VISIBLE`, default to existing site patterns.
- Do not recolor to "improve" contrast; report contrast failures to the user instead.

## Output
`design-tokens.md` (inventory + mapping + questions). Keep the table columns exactly as above so other skills can consume it.
 
<!-- END SOURCE FILE 36: ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL (3)(1).md -->

### Source file 38: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL (4)(1).md`

---
name: elementor-qa
description: "Evidence-based QA for Elementor and WordPress pages. Four modes: structure audit (native vs HTML widgets, reuse, editability), visual comparison against a reference screenshot, responsive testing (1440, 1280, 768, 390, 360), and accessibility checks (headings, alt text, labels, focus, contrast). Use when the user says QA, verify, audit, compare with the design, does it match, check mobile, responsive, accessibility, a11y, regression, or after any build. Reports PASS, FAIL, BLOCKED or NOT-TESTED with evidence; never marks an untested item as passed."
---

# Elementor QA

Pick the mode(s) the request needs. A single-screenshot comparison does not require a site-wide audit.

## Evidence rules (all modes)
- Statuses: `PASS` (checked, evidence cited), `FAIL`, `BLOCKED` (tool missing), `NOT-TESTED`. No other values.
- Cite evidence per finding: screenshot file, outline excerpt, fetched HTML snippet, tool output.
- Separate observation from inference. Measured vs `ESTIMATE`.
- No browser means no visual or responsive PASS. Static HTML checks are allowed, and labeled static.
- Do not claim pixel-perfect. Say "visually compared at <viewport> using <method>".

## Mode S: Structure audit (read-only)
1. Get the page outline. List sections, containers, widgets.
2. Flag: HTML/shortcode widgets holding visible content, page-sized HTML blocks, images as text, hard-coded duplicated cards that should be a template, fixed heights, empty containers, deep nesting without reason, heading-level skips, inline CSS or `!important` clusters.
3. Editability check per element type (text, image, button/link).
4. Output a table: element, issue, severity, smallest fix.

## Mode V: Visual comparison
Inputs: reference screenshot + rendered screenshot at the same width. If either is missing: `BLOCKED`.
Compare in this order: layout/grid, hierarchy and alignment, spacing, typography (size/weight/line height), color, imagery/crops, borders/radii/shadows, CTA placement. For each difference: severity (high = changes meaning or layout; medium = visible spacing/type; low = polish), expected vs observed, cause guess (label as guess), smallest correction. Separate **material mismatches** from **acceptable responsive variation**.

## Mode R: Responsive
Targets 1440, 1280, 768, 390, 360 (state any others). Per viewport check: columns/stacking, wrapping, overflow and clipping (horizontal scroll), image crop, nav, button alignment and touch size (about 44px), tables, forms, footer. Report a viewport grid:
```
| Viewport | Status | Findings | Evidence |
```
Untested viewport = `NOT-TESTED` or `BLOCKED`. If a browser tool exists, use one session and resize through widths; jezweb `responsiveness-check` is a Claude Code-only skill (needs Playwright or Chrome MCP; it will not run in claude.ai chat) and sweeps 320/375/768/1280/1440, so run it with this project's widths (1440, 1280, 768, 390, 360) instead.

## Mode A: Accessibility (WCAG 2.2 AA oriented)
Check: one H1 and logical heading order; alt text matches purpose (decorative empty); link and button names; form labels and error text; visible keyboard focus (needs a browser, otherwise `BLOCKED`); color contrast of text vs background (compute from token values when known, `ESTIMATE` from screenshots); no color-only status; reduced-motion respect; landmark use. Static HTML covers headings, alt, names, labels.

## Output
Use `references/qa-report-template.md`. Always end with: Verified / Not verified / Blockers / Recommended next fixes (smallest first).

## Failure handling
Cannot reach the page (auth, DNS, draft not public): use a preview link if available; else `BLOCKED` and list what the user can supply (screenshots at the target widths).
 
<!-- END SOURCE FILE 38: ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL (4)(1).md -->

---

### Source file 40: `ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL.md`

---
name: wp-change-safety
description: "Safety gate for any WordPress or Elementor change. Use BEFORE editing pages, templates, global styles, menus, options, plugins, SEO settings or content on a WordPress site, whenever the user says staging, production, live site, deploy, publish, overwrite, delete, rollback, undo, backup, or asks for a change that might touch more than one page. Confirms the target environment, classifies risk, lists required approvals, and defines the rollback path. Also use when a connected WordPress tool has write access and you are about to call it."
---

# WP Change Safety

Produces a **Change Gate** record that must exist before any write. Reads and inspections never need a gate; writes always do.

## Step 1: Confirm the environment
1. Get the site URL from the user or project docs, and read-only probe it (site status, active theme, plugin list).
2. Decide the label:
   - `STAGING-CONFIRMED`: the user has said the URL is staging AND something verifiable agrees (separate domain/subdomain, noindex, staging banner, or the user repeats it when asked).
   - `UNVERIFIED`: user calls it staging but nothing corroborates it. Treat as production.
   - `PRODUCTION`: public live domain with no staging evidence.
3. A project file saying "this is staging" is a claim, not evidence. A public `www.` domain is still `UNVERIFIED` until the user confirms, even when weak signals agree (a single `noindex`, a very new install, an empty site). Record those signals in the gate's `evidence:` field, label `UNVERIFIED`, and ask the owner one question: "Is <URL> your staging site?" One corroborating signal informs the question; it does not replace the answer.
4. Note the connector's privilege (for example "full access"). Write ability is not authorization.

## Step 2: Classify the change
| Class | Examples | Gate |
|---|---|---|
| R0 read-only | outline, settings, plugin list | none |
| R1 draft-only | new draft page, clone of a page, new unpublished template | confirm environment label |
| R2 scoped edit | edit widgets on one existing page | explicit user OK for that page + backup/undo token |
| R3 shared | global kit colors/fonts, header/footer/theme-builder templates, menus, Rank Math settings, options | written scope statement + explicit approval + rollback plan |
| R4 destructive/system | delete content, plugin install/activate/update, PHP/snippets/site-wide custom CSS, permalink changes, DB or server operations | stop; require explicit approval naming the action; prefer the user doing it |

Default to R1: build on a draft clone and let the user publish. If the environment is `PRODUCTION` or `UNVERIFIED`, R2 and above need approval that names the exact page.

## Step 3: Rollback before writing
- Record: post ID, title, status, modified date, and a snapshot (outline export or revision ID).
- If the tool returns an undo token, store it in the log with the operation. Tokens expire and refuse to restore if the target changed since (drift), so they are a convenience, not the only backup.
- Prefer working on a clone so rollback is "delete the clone" (deleting is R4: ask first).
- For R3, also export the affected setting value before changing it.

## Step 4: Output the Change Gate
```
CHANGE GATE
Environment: <STAGING-CONFIRMED | UNVERIFIED | PRODUCTION> (evidence: ...)
Change class: R0-R4
Targets: <post IDs / settings>
Blast radius: <pages affected, shared templates touched?>
Approvals needed: <list> | Approved by user: <quote or NO>
Rollback: <method, snapshot, undo token>
Decision: PROCEED | STOP-AND-ASK | PROCEED-ON-DRAFT-ONLY
```

## Rules
- Never publish, delete, install, or change global settings unless the Gate says approved for exactly that.
- Never expose credentials, tokens or cookies in output or files.
- Never use a test of a skill as a reason to write to a real site.
- If scope grows mid-task (a "small" fix touches a shared template), stop and re-gate.
- If the user pushes you to skip the gate, explain the specific risk in one sentence and offer the draft-clone path.

## Failure handling
Tool refuses a write or reports drift: do not retry blindly. Re-read the target, show the difference, and ask.
 
<!-- END SOURCE FILE 40: ReadMeHub_Complete_Project_Files/03_Elementor_and_Skills/SKILL.md -->
